2 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
2.1

BACKGROUND

On June 24th, 2004 a not approvable letter was issued due to a lack of evidence of effectiveness in the
short or long term treatment of major depressive disorder.
This not approvable letter also requested the following:
•

Comparative dissolution data in multiple media in order to request a biowaiver for a change in
tablet shape (biconvex to modified flat) for the 40 mg and 60 mg strengths.

and agreement to perform three phase IV commitments:
a) In vivo effect of an intermediate inducer such as rifabutin
b) Effects of different meal compositions
c) Re-evaluation of the 12 hour dissolution specification after production of 20 batches of each
strength
A type C Guidance Meeting was subsequently held on May 31, 2006. In this meeting the sponsor
indicated that the embossment on the original modified flat tablet was being changed. It was agreed that
comparative dissolution information in multiple media linking the modified flat tablet with the to-bemarketed embossment to the modified flat tablet with the original embossment, and to the original
biconvex tablet would be provided in the complete response.

2.2

RECOMMENDATIONS

The Office of Clinical Pharmacology / Division of Clinical Pharmacology - 1 (OCP/DCP-1) has reviewed
the Complete Response to the Not Approvable letter dated June for NDA #21-164 AZ submitted May 1,
2007.
OCP finds this application acceptable providing agreement can be reached on labeling and so long as the
sponsor provides written agreement to a phase IV commitment to perform a drug-drug interaction study of
the effect of an intermediate CYP 3A4 inducer such as rifabutin within 1 year of approval.
OCP also grants a waiver to the requirement for a bioequivalence study for the proposed 40 mg and 60
mg tablet strengths.
OCP recommends that the regulatory dissolution method and specifications be set if the product is
approved. The previous recommendation that an interim specification be set and that there be a phase IV
commitment to re-evaluate the specification for the 12 hour sampling time after the production of 20
batches of each strength should be disregarded.
Comments should be communicated to the sponsor as appropriate:
(See section 3.1 on page 3).
Phase IV commitments should be communicated to the sponsor as appropriate:
(See section 3.2 on page 4).
Labeling comments should also be communicated to the sponsor as appropriate:
(See section 3.3 on page 5).

Last printed 10/1/2007 3:59:00
Page 2 of 77

2.3

SUMMARY OF IMPORTANT FINDINGS
1) Phase IV commitment to study the in vivo effect of an intermediate inducer such as
rifabutin.

The sponsor has not made this commitment and will be requested again.
2) Phase IV committment to study the effects of different meal compositions.
This study has been completed and the results are acceptable.
The magnitude of the food effect was clearly dependent on the fat content of the breakfast. The peak
concentration of gepirone after intake of low-fat breakfast was 27% higher, after medium-fat breakfast
55% higher and after a high-fat breakfast 62% higher as compared to the fasted state.
The total exposure to gepirone also increased with the fat content of the breakfast with the AUC0-tlast
after intake of low-fat breakfast was 14% higher, after a medium-fat breakfast 22% higher and after a
high-fat breakfast 37% higher as compared to the fasted state. For the AUC∞ similar results were
found, i.e., 14%, 18% and 32% respectively.
The median Tmax was significantly shortened after intake of the medium-fat (3 hrs) and high-fat
(3 hrs) meals compared to the fasted state (5 hrs), and was also shorter for the low-fat meal, (4 hrs).
The effect of varying amounts of fat on Cmax and AUC of the metabolites 3’-OH-gepirone and 1-PP
has basically the same pattern as was found for gepirone itself although the magnitude of the effect is
attenuated, resulting in sometimes a statistically significant effect and sometimes a statistically nonsignificant effect.
3) Comparative dissolution data in multiple media in order to request a biowaiver for a
change in tablet shape (biconvex to modified flat) for the 40 mg and 60 mg strengths, and
a change in embossment for all strengths.
Dissolution of all strengths of the final proposed Fabre-Kramer embossed flat tablet are acceptable and
biowaivers should be granted.
4) Phase IV committment to re-evaluate of the 12 hour dissolution specification after
production of 20 batches of each strength.
The dissolution method and specifications can be set now based upon the two pivotal clinical batches and
a phase IV commitment is not appropriate or necessary.

3 COMMENTS
3.1

COMMENTS TO THE SPONSOR
1) All strengths of the modified flat tablet with the Fabre-Kramer embossment are acceptable based
upon the submitted comparative dissolution data, a biowaiver is granted, and a bioequivalence
study is not needed.
2) The sponsor is requested to adopt the following as the regulatory dissolution method and
specifications, (see Table 1). A phase IV commitment to re-evaluate the 12 hour dissolution
specification is not necessary or appropriate as specifications are being set based upon
dissolution at release for pivotal clinical batches. (This is the typical practice.)

PHASE IV COMMITMENTS TO BE COMMUNICATED TO THE SPONSOR
1) The sponsor needs to provide written agreement to a phase IV commitment to perform a drugdrug interaction study of the effect of an intermediate CYP 3A4 inducer such as rifabutin within 1
year of approval.
2) The sponsor should conduct studies to investigate the potential for dose-dumping in the presence
of alcohol for all strengths of extended release gepirone tablets. The dissolution conditions shown
in the following table should be used.

in the regulatory media was also acceptable and are shown in the following sections.

5.3

RE-EVALUATION OF REGULATORY DISSOLUTION SPECIFICATIONS

In the present submission the sponsor indicates that the proposed regulatory dissolution specifications
were based on an analysis of all drug batches and stability data. This is inappropriate as stability data is
intended to verify that the drug product will behave the same as the pivotal clinical batches over the entire
product shelf-life.
Consequently, if approval is granted the regulatory dissolution specifications will be based upon the two
pivotal efficacy studies.
The two pivotal efficacy studies and the gepirone ER tablets used in these studies are shown in Table 10,
and more detailed information about the batches used in these studies is shown in Table 11.
The dissolution data for these two batches are shown in Table 12. Although the method is not mentioned,
it is assumed that the currently proposed method in 0.01 M HCl was used as the mean dissolution values
are similar to what has been reported previously with this methodology and since a different method was
unlikely to have been employed, expecially for study FKGBE007.
Based upon the mean dissolution values for these two batches final dissolution specifications can be set
as follows:

5.4

EFFECT OF FOOD AND MEAL COMPOSITION

I concur with the sponsor’s conclusions regarding the effect of various meal compositions on the
pharmacokinetics of Gepirone HCl MR tablets which is shown in the following italicized text with
supporting data in Table 13 - Table 15 on the following pages:
”An effect of food was found for the peak concentration (Cmax) of gepirone and the total exposure
(AUC0-tlast, AUC0∞) to gepirone. The magnitude of the food effect was clearly dependent of the fat
content of the breakfast. The peak concentration of gepirone after intake of low-fat breakfast was 27%
higher, after medium-fat breakfast 55% higher and after high-fat breakfast 62% higher as compared to
the fasted state.
The total exposure to gepirone increased with the fat content of the breakfast: the AUC0-tlast after
intake of low-fat breakfast was 14% higher, after medium-fat breakfast 22% higher and after high-fat
breakfast 37% higher as compared to the fasted state. For the AUC0-∞ similar results were found,
i.e., 14%, 18% and 32% respectively.
The time of occurrence of the peak concentration (tmax) was significantly decreased after intake of
medium-fat (3 h) and high-fat (3 h) meals compared to the fasted state (5 h).
The effect of varying amounts of fat on Cmax and AUC of the metabolites 3’-OH-gepirone and 1-PP
has basically the same pattern as was found for gepirone itself although the magnitude of the effect is
attenuated, resulting in sometimes a statistically significant effect and sometimes a statistically nonsignificant effect.”
This was single-dose, randomized, four-way cross-over study of the effect of various fed conditions,
(fasted, low fat, medium fat, and high fat), on the pharmacokinetics of gepirone HCl from ER tablets and
the pharmacokinetics of the 3’OH and 1-PP [1-(2-Pyrimidinyl) piperazine] metabolites in 32 healthy male
Caucasian volunteers 18 – 45 years of age who are phenotypically CYP2D6 extensive metabolizers by a
dextromethorphan test.
Subjects were hospitalized from the afternoon/evening of Day -1 until the morning of Day 3 in each
treatment period.
The following treatments were given in randomized order with an interperiod washout of at least 1 week.
•

Treatment A - 20 mg Gepirone HCl extended release (ER) tablets under fasting conditions.

•

Treatment B - 20 mg Gepirone HCl ER tablets after consumption of a high-fat breakfast.

•

Treatment C - 20 mg Gepirone HCl ER tablets after consumption of a medium-fat breakfast.

•

Treatment D - 20 mg Gepirone HCl ER tablets after consumption of a low-fat breakfast.

There were no serious AEs in this trial, and none of the subjects discontinued from the trial due to AEs.
Eleven subjects (34.4%) had one or more AEs during the trial. Headache (eight reports by five subjects)
and dizziness (seven reports by three subjects) were the most commonly reported AEs. The incidence,
intensity and time pattern (i.e., onset time and duration) for both events was similar for the various
treatments.
As the single dose study of the lowest dose the effects this does not represent the clinical effects with
clinical dosing, however this was addressed in a prior OCP review and was found acceptable.
This was the second study that the sponsor performed, in the first study (28725) it was found upon
analysis that the fat content of the meals was incorrect. Thus the study was repeated. Prior to conducting
the repeat study the meals were analyzed and the high-fat, high-caloric meal was found to be deficient in
calories and fat content. Thus the meal was modified and the protocol amended. Because of all of the

C:\dmautop\temp\CKavanagh DataReviewsNDA21-164 Gepirone ResubmitReviewFinal21-164 AZ 1-May-07 CR - OCP Review.doc
Last printed 10/1/2007 3:59:00
Page 72 of 77

problems with the meal contents this reviewer attempted to verify caloric contents. The analysis of the
meal composition were not in English and insufficient data was provided to completely verify the final high
fat content meal, however by assuming that the final high fat content meal only differed in the amount of
fat as mentioned by the sponsor it was found that the final caloric content as calculated by the reviewer
roughly matches that reported by the sponsor. The final compositions of the meals used are shown in
Table 16 and are acceptable.
In spite of all the difficulties with meal content the results of this study are almost identical to the previous
study (82725) where the meal composition was found to be deficient..

COMMITMENTS TO PERFORM PREVIOUSLY PROPOSED PHASE IV STUDIES
RE-EVALUATION OF 12 HOUR DISSOLUTION SPECIFICATION

Fabre-Kramer agreed in their cover memo, however there is no need for the sponsor to perform this commitment as specifications need to be
based on release data of pivotal clinical batches and not on production batches or stability data.
5.5.2

MEASUREMENT OF THE EFFECT OF INTERMEDIATE INDUCERS SUCH AS RIFABUTIN

There was no mention of this in the cover memo where the sponsor addresses other requested phase IV commitments and there is no obvious
place to find such a commitment elsewhere in the submission. Consequently, it is assumed that the sponsor has not commited to perform this
proposed phase IV study. This is a clinically appropriate request and if the product is to be approved the sponsor should be asked to commit to
this study prior to approval.

5.6

NEW ADDITIONAL PROPOSED PHASE IV STUDIES

OCP has recommended that the potental of alcohol to induce dose-dumping be examined as a phase IV commitment, and this reviewer concurs.
OCP has requested that the conditions to be used are the tentative conditions that have been proposed by the FDA working group. This reviewer
acquieses to this request, otherwise this reviewer would normally suggest alcohol concentrations in 4% increments with investigation of additional
intermediate alcohol concentrations.

Executive Summary

This review evaluates the Sponsor’s response to the recommendations made by the Office
of Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics (OCPB) in the not approvable Action
Letter (issued June 28, 2002) for NDA 21-346.
The Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics Recommendations for that submission
were not related to the not approvable deficiencies. The action letter noted that these
recommendations should be addressed if the Sponsor wished to re-submit the application.
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics (OCPB) recommended the
following:
•

•

•

•

The pivotal bioequivalence study did not show that the 40 and 80 mg strengths
were bioequivalent to the 20 mg strength tablet that was used in the clinical trials.
No adequate data were provided comparing the 60 mg strength to the 20 mg
strength. The 40, 60, and 80 mg strengths were not approvable.
The food effect study showed a significant effect on gepirone bioavailability
(increase in Cmax approximately 62%). The Sponsor should conduct an in vivo
study evaluating the effects of different meal composition on gepirone ER
pharmacokinetics.
A strong pharmacokinetic interaction was observed between gepirone and
ketoconazole (5-fold increase in gepirone concentrations). The Sponsor should
conduct an in vivo drug interaction study to evaluate the effects of gepirone coadministration with an intermediate inhibitor of CYP3A4, such as verapamil.
The Sponsor should conduct an in vitro assessment of (a) potential drug-drug
interactions between gepirone and potent CYP3A4 inducers and (b) the drug’s
ability to induce CYP3A4 enzymes.

1

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
•
•
•

The Sponsor should conduct an in vivo drug-drug interaction study with a potent
CYP2D6 inhibitor to assess its effects on the pharmacokinetics of gepirone and its
metabolites.
The dissolution specification at 12 h and 20 h should be changed to 65-85% and >
85%, respectively.
The Sponsor should clarify whether or not plasma gepirone (and any metabolites)
concentrations were measured in any of the pivotal efficacy trials following
administration of the IR and/or ER formulations of gepirone.

1.1

Recommendations

The Sponsor has provided studies or justification to address the recommendations of the
Office of Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics that were identified in the not
approvable letter. Although the Sponsor’s responses were generally acceptable, there are
several recently identified issues regarding the pharmacokinetics as well as recent
changes in the drug product that are of some concern.
•

The 20 and 80 mg strength tablets (with the modified flat tablet shape) are
acceptable, but the 40 and 80 mg strength tablets (with the modified flat tablet
shape) are not acceptable.
The nonequivalence of the 40 and 80 mg strength tablets compared to the
approved 20 mg strength of gepirone would most likely have little impact on
the adverse event profile. A biowaiver for the 60 mg strength as originally
manufactured could be granted. However, the Sponsor has now changed the
tablet shape with a change in commercial tooling from the original biconvex
tablet to the modified flat tablets. Dissolution has only been evaluated for the
20 mg and 80 mg strength tablets. Therefore the 40 and 60 mg strength
tablets (with the modified flat tablet shape) are not considered approvable at
this time.
• The Sponsor should submit results of dissolution comparisons (of
the original vs. modified shape) in multiple media with f2
comparisons to request a biowaiver for the 40 and 60 mg strength
tablets.

•

The Sponsor’s proposed dissolution specifications are acceptable on an interim
basis. The dissolution specifications are as follows:
15-25% at 1 h
40-85% at 5 h
65-86% at 12 h
> 86% at 20 h
These specifications are acceptable provided that the Sponsor adheres to the
stated commitment to re-evaluate the specification at 12 hours after the
manufacture of 20 batches of each strength.
1.2

•
•

Recommended Phase 4 Study Commitments

The effect of intermediate inducers such as rifabutin should be evaluated as a
Phase IV commitment.
The Sponsor has previously agreed to conduct an in vivo study evaluating the
effects of different meal compositions on gepirone ER pharmacokinetics as a
Phase IV commitment and states that the study has been initiated.

3

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
•

The Sponsor has stated a commitment to re-evaluate the specification at 12 hours
after the manufacture of 20 batches of each strength.

The proposed labeling is acceptable from the OCPB perspective provided that the
Sponsor make the recommended changes. Labeling comments can be found in section 4.
Please forward these comments as well as the labeling comments in Section 4 to the
Sponsor.

Comments to Medical Officer (do not send to Sponsor)
•

In the original submission, a significant pharmacokinetic interaction was observed
between ketoconazole, a potent inhibitor of CYP3A4, and gepirone (a 5-fold
increase in gepirone AUC). To minimize the risk of toxicity, we recommend that
coadministration with ketoconazole or other potent inhibitors of CYP3A4 be
considered as a warning.

•

A significant pharmacokinetic interaction was observed between gepirone and
rifampin, a potent inducer of CYP3A4 (a 20-fold decrease in Cmax and a 29-fold
decrease in AUC for gepirone). Because of the risk of reduced efficacy, we
recommend that coadministration with rifampin and other potent inducers should
be contraindicated.

•

A significant pharmacokinetic interaction was observed between gepirone and
verapamil, an intermediate inhibitor of CYP3A4 (a 2.6 fold increase in Cmax and
AUC was observed). To minimize the risk of toxicity, when verapamil and
gepirone are co-administered, gepirone should be used cautiously. A low dose of
gepirone is recommended with subsequent dosage adjustment based on clinical
assessment. We recommend that the dose of gepirone in these patients not exceed
40 mg/day.

•

As noted in the original submission, gepirone concentrations are increased 5080% in the elderly. However, exposure depends on other factors as well (such as
race for which there is a 1.5-2-fold decrease in AUC in Blacks and Asians
compared to Caucasians). To minimize the risk of toxicity in the elderly we
recommend that dosing in each patient should be individualized. Dosing of
(b) (4)
gepirone in the elderly should be started at the lowest strength and
.

•

In the original submission it was noted that plasma concentrations of gepirone and
its metabolites were elevated by approximately 70% in patients with moderate
and severe renal impairment. To minimize the risk of toxicity in patients with
moderate or severe renal impairment we recommend that dosing in each patient

4

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
should be individualized. Dosing of gepirone in patients with moderate or severe
(b) (4)
renal impairment should be started at the lowest strength and
.
•

In the original submission it was noted that gepirone exposure is augmented 2fold in patients with moderately impaired hepatic function. To minimize the risk
of toxicity, gepirone should be used cautiously in patients with moderately
impaired hepatic function. A low dose of gepirone is recommended with
subsequent dosage adjustment based on clinical assessment. We recommend that
the dose of gepirone in these patients not exceed 40 mg/day.

•

The pharmacokinetics of gepirone have not been adequately studied in patients
with severe hepatic impairment. Therefore we recommend that the drug not be
administered in this population and that there should be a warning or a
contraindication regarding use of gepirone in this population.

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics Findings
3.1

Background

NDA 21-346 for Gepirone HCl (Org 33062) extended release (ER) tablets for use in the
treatment of major depressive disorder was submitted May 18, 2001. Gepirone is a
relatively selective partial agonist at serotonergic 5-HT1A receptors with lower affinity
for 5-HT1B and other CNS receptors. In addition, 3’-OH gepirone, a metabolite of
gepirone, has antidepressant activity as well. The recommended starting dose was to be
20 mg/day. The minimum efficacious dose was thought to be 40 mg. The dose could be
increased to a maximum dose of 80 mg/day.
According to the OCPB review of February 19, 2002 the pharmacokinetic characteristics
of gepirone are as follows:
•
•
•
•
•

•

Poor oral absorption; F=14-17%
Linear PK for ER tablets at 20-80 mg doses (high intra- and inter-subject
variability)
Steady state PK: approaches nonlinearity at higher doses
Terminal half life: 4.5-12 h following ER administration
Metabolism: 1-PP and 3’OH-geprione are both present at higher plasma levels
than parent drug
o Mediated via CYP3A4 and to a lesser extent 2D6
o Other hydroxylated metabolites present in urine; mainly 3;5-dihydroxygepirone
Elimination: 81% of administered dose in urine and 13% in feces

NDA 21-346 received a not approvable Action Letter (issued March 14, 2002). The nonapproval deficiencies were related to inadequate efficacy data and inadequate safety data.
OCPB review of that submission resulted in the following comments and requests to the
Sponsor:
•

•

•

The pivotal bioequivalence study did not show that the 40 and 80 mg strengths
were bioequivalent to the 20 mg strength tablet that was used in the clinical trials.
No adequate data were provided comparing the 60 mg strength to the 20 mg
strength. The 40, 60, and 80 mg strengths were not approvable.
The food effect study showed a significant effect on gepirone bioavailability
(increase in Cmax approximately 62%). The Sponsor should conduct an in vivo
study evaluating the effects of different meal composition on gepirone ER
pharmacokinetics.
A strong pharmacokinetic interaction was observed between gepirone and
ketoconazole (5-fold increase in gepirone concentrations). The Sponsor should
conduct an in vivo drug interaction study to evaluate the effects of gepirone coadministration with an intermediate inhibitor of CYP3A4, such as verapamil.
7

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
•
•
•
•

The Sponsor should conduct an in vitro assessment of (a) potential drug-drug
interactions between gepirone and potent CYP3A4 inducers and (b) the drug’s
ability to induce CYP3A4 enzymes.
The Sponsor should conduct an in vivo drug-drug interaction study with a potent
CYP2D6 inhibitor to assess its effects on the pharmacokinetics of gepirone and its
metabolites.
The dissolution specification at 12 h and 20 h should be changed to 65-85% and >
85%, respectively.
The Sponsor should clarify whether or not plasma gepirone (and any metabolites)
concentrations were measured in any of the pivotal efficacy trials following
administration of the IR and/or ER formulations of gepirone.

In the present review, OCPB has evaluated the following responses to OCPB comments
that were submitted by the Sponsor in this resubmission:
•
•
•
•
•
•
•

Evaluation of Safety data to consider whether there is likely to be a clinically
relevant difference in safety due to the difference in exposure due to the 4x20 mg
tablets compared to exposure after administration of the 80 mg strength tablets.
Dissolution studies to support request for biowaiver of 60 mg strength

o Includes assessment of gepirone’s ability to induce or inhibit CYP3A4

The Sponsor has initiated a food effect study, and as previously agreed by the Agency, it
would be part of a Phase IV commitment.

3.2.1

Current Submission

Justification for approval of the 40 and 80 mg tablet strengths

Are the 40 and 80 mg strengths approvable?
The pivotal bioequivalence study compared the bioavailability of 2x 40 mg with 2x20 mg
and 1x 80 with 4x20 mg tablets. The results of that study indicated that the
bioequivalence criterion was not met on Cmax and AUC for the comparison between 1x
40 mg vs 2x20 mg (90% C.I. for Cmax: 1.02-1.35, 90% CI for AUC: 0.99-1.33). In
addition the Cmax of the 1x80 mg strength was significantly higher than the 4x20 mg
tablets (90% CI for Cmax: 0.96-1.26).
The Sponsor’s approach to justify the approval of the 40 and 80 mg strength tablets was
to evaluating the relationship between dose or plasma concentrations and safety
measures, bracketing the plasma concentrations from these strengths with higher
exposures due to drug interactions or doses greater than 80 mg. The Sponsor has
provided the following two considerations:
•

Plasma concentration-adverse event (AE) relationship in healthy subjects
The Sponsor determined the relationship between exposure (Cmax and AUC) and
intensity of AE in healthy male subjects using data from Study 28717
(ketoconazole interaction study) and Study 28718 (pivotal BE Study). (Both
studies were reviewed in the original submission of the NDA). Of note, due to
the ketoconazole interaction with gepirone, Cmax and AUC were approximately
40% higher in study 28717 than in Study 28718 for a given 2x20 mg dose. The
mean results are shown in the Tables below. A positive relationship between
exposure parameters and AE intensity for dizziness and nausea is observed. In
comparing the two studies, Study 28718 showed lower Cmax and AUC values
compared with values observed in study 28717 at comparable AE intensities.

Table 1 Mean (SD) values for Cmax and AUC by AE intensity for dizziness (Study 28717) after a

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
The Sponsor has concluded that a 30% increase in Cmax and AUC for subjects not
experiencing AEs will not lead to a moderate or severe AE intensity.
•

Dose-AE relationship in patients with depression

The sponsor conducted an analysis to investigate the relationship between dose and
adverse events. A logistic regression model was fit to AE data collected in 8 clinical
trials in patients with depression. The six adverse events included in the model were
dizziness, headache, insomnia, nausea, nervousness, and somnolence. The doses of
gepirone ranged from 20 to 80 mg across studies.
The final model was used to predict the increased risk for a given AE due to a 17%
increase in exposure when switching from 4 x 20 mg to 1 x 80 mg ER tablets. This was
accomplished by evaluating the risk of a particular AE for a 93.6 mg dose, which was
calculated as the “exposure equivalent” dose to the 1 x 80 mg dosage strength. The
logistic regression model predicted a 7% increase in dizziness and less than 3% increase
for insomnia and nausea.
OCPB review of the concentration-AE relationship attempts to bracket the exposure after
the 1x80 mg tablets and 4x20 mg tablet with exposures known to result in AEs. Based on
plasma concentrations observed in phase I studies, the highest exposure to drug occurred
in Study 28717 during concomitant administration of a single dose of 40 mg (2x20 mg)
ER tablets with 200 mg ketoconazole. In that treatment group 100% of the subjects
experienced dizziness, 92% experienced nausea, and 67% vomited. The Cmax values for
this treatment group ranged from 17.3-42.8 ng/ml. In comparison, co-administration of
20 mg gepirone with 200 mg ketoconazole resulted in greater than 50% of subjects
experiencing dizziness and nausea; 8% of subjects vomited.
A plot of the mean (±sd) concentration-time profiles for 1 x 80 mg and 4 x 20 mg ER
tablets administered as a single dose to 32 healthy males (study 28718) shows that both
profiles fall within the range of concentrations known to cause AEs (Figure 1).
Furthermore, the plot shows that the variability for both formulations is comparable, even
at peak concentrations.

40

Since gepirone will be used to treat a chronic condition, the difference between the two
formulations at steady-state is more clinically relevant than the differences following a
single dose. Nonparametric superposition was used to simulate the mean steady-state
concentrations for the 1 x 80 mg and 4 x 20 mg ER tablets (Figure 2). Predicted steady
state concentrations for each formulation are very similar, with mean peak concentrations
of 11.1 ng/ml for the 1 x 80 mg tablets and 10.4 ng/ml for the 2 x 40 mg tablets. Total
predicted exposures, as measured by AUC over the dosing interval, for each formulation
are 153 ng.h/ml and 155 ng.h/ml, respectively.

Are the 40 and 80 mg strengths approvable? OCPB concurs with the Sponsor that the
nonequivalence of the to-be-marketed strengths compared to the approved 20 mg strength
of gepirone will most likely have little impact on the adverse event profile. Thus 80 mg
strength can be considered approvable. In addition, since the 40 mg strength is bracketed
by the 20 and 80 mg strengths, it can also be considered approvable.
3.2.2

Justification for Approval of the 60 mg tablet strength

Is there data to support the approval of the 60 mg tablet strength?
The Sponsor has requested a biowaiver for the 60 mg strength Org 33062 ER tablets. For
a full review please refer to Appendix 5, Section 5.2.2.
The composition of the 60 mg tablet compared to the 20 mg tablet difference in the
(b) (4)
(b)
. The difference is (4) % relative to the total weight of
(b)
(b) (4)
the 20 mg tablet. Since this falls within the SUPAC MR level (4) changes for
, in vitro documentation requires dissolution profile comparison in
multiple media.
The Sponsor has carried out dissolution testing for all tablet strengths in multiple media
(0.01 M HCl, pH 4.5 buffer, pH 6.8 buffer, water, and 0.1 M HCl). The 0.01 N HCl is
the medium used in the method that was found acceptable in the OCPB review of the
original NDA.

11

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
The results of the dissolution profile comparisons of the 20 mg and 60 mg tablet strengths
showed f2 values between 50 -100 in each of the media tested. In addition the f1 values
were between 0-15 in all cases. For the 40 and 80 mg tablets, the f1 and f2 test results
were also in acceptable ranges, in comparison to the 20 mg strength tablets, in all media.
A biowaiver can be granted for the 60 mg strength tablets based on similarity of the in
vitro dissolution profiles in multiple media in comparison to the 20 mg strength tablet. In
addition, the 40 and 80 mg strength tablets showed similarity in all in vitro dissolution
conditions to the 20 mg strength tablets.
The studies for the biowaiver were performed with the original biconvex tablet shape.
Subsequently, the Sponsor change the shape of the tablets from biconvex to flat. In order
to be acceptable for commercial use, a comparative dissolution study should be
performed. Such a study was performed for the 20 and 80 mg strengths, but not for the
proposed 40 and 60 mg strengths. Please see section 3.2.8 for a review of these and other
changes in the drug product, and resulting recommendations.
3.2.3

Verapamil Interaction Study (Study 28724)

What effect does an intermediate inhibitor of CYP3A4 have on Org 33062
pharmacokinetics?
The Sponsor has submitted the results of Study 28724 that evaluated the effect of
verapamil, an intermediate inhibitor of CCYP3A4, on the pharmacokinetics of Org 33062
in healthy male subjects ages 21-44 years old. This study was an open-label,
randomized, 2-period, 2-treatment, 2-sequence crossover study design with multiple
doses of verapamil and single doses of Org 33062, as shown in Table 1, below.

These results show a greater than two-fold increase in systemic exposure to gepirone

3.2.4

Can the metabolism of gepirone via CYP3A4 be induced?
The Sponsor evaluated whether gepirone CYP3A4-mediated metabolism could be

crossover study design with multiple doses of rifampicin and multiple doses of Org
33062, as shown in the Table below. There was a washout of at least 4 weeks between

These results show a decrease in exposure to gepirone of approximately 20-fold and 29fold as determined by Cmax and AUC, respectively, after exposure to rifampin, with an
approximate 29-fold increase in apparent oral clearance. There were also decreases in
exposure observed for the 3’-OH gepirone metabolite (an approximate 2.5-fold decrease
in Cmax, a 2.9-fold decrease in AUC, and a 2.9-fold increase in apparent oral clearance),
but much smaller changes were observed for the 1-PP metabolite. The 90% confidence
intervals (0.037 to 0.07 for Cmax, 0.024 to 0.048 for AUC, and 19.6 to 43.9 for Cl)
indicate a significant interaction.
Co-administration of gepirone with rifampin resulted in significantly reduced exposure to
gepirone that could result in therapeutic failure. Similar reduction in gepirone exposure
is expected with other potent CYP3A4 inducers.
Does gepirone inhibit or induce CYP3A4?
The ratio of 6β-hydroxycoritsol to free cortisol in urine was used as a marker for
CYP3A4 activity, in which a higher ratio would indicate higher CYP3A4 activity. For
this evaluation there were 20 evaluable subjects with complete data. For Gepirone alone,

15

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
the point estimate for the ratio of post/pre-treatment (Day 7/8: Day -1/1) is 1.03 (90%
confidence interval 0.85-1.26). For gepirone + rifampin the point estimate for the ratio of
post/pre-treatment is 4.62 (90% confidence interval 3.83-5.57). These results confirm
that induction occurred in the presence of rifampin, and suggest that gepirone alone did
not induce CYP3A4. However, the dose of gepirone used was not the maximal planned
dose of gepirone, and therefore an effect at a higher dose cannot be ruled out.
3.2.5

In Vivo Paroxetine-Mediated CYP2D6 inhibition Study (Study 28703)

Can a potent CYP2D6 inhibitor (paroxetine) result in significantly increased exposure
when co-administered with Org 33062?
Study 28703 evaluated the effects of multiple oral dosing of paroxetine on the steady
state pharmacokinetics of Org 33062 and vice versa. This study was double-blind,
multiple oral dose, randomized, three-period cross-over pharmacokinetic interaction trial
of Org 33062 and paroxetine. The study was performed in 30 healthy male and female
subjects from 20-45 years old who were extensive metabolizers of CYP2D6. The
treatments were as follows:
Treatment A: oral administration of 20 mg Org 33062 once daily on Days 1 and 2,
40 mg Org 33062 once daily on Days 3 to 8 and paroxetine placebo Days
1-9
Treatment B: oral administration of 20 mg paroxetine once daily on Days 1-9
Org 33062 placebo on Days 1-8
Treatment C: oral administration of 20 mg Org 33062 once daily on Days 1 and 2, 40
mg Org 33062 once daily on Days 3-8 and 20 mg paroxetine once daily on
Days 1-9.

pertinent pharmacokinetic parameters for gepirone, 3’-OH-gepirone, and 1-PP are shown

This study indicates that gepirone at a 40 mg dose does not inhibit the metabolism of

effect on CYP2D6 as this is not the maximum planned dose.
3.2.6

Other In Vivo Drug-Interaction Studies

Are there any other in vivo drug-drug interaction studies that indicate the exposure to
Org 33062 when given alone is different when drugs are co-administered?
The Sponsor has conducted drug-drug interaction study to investigate the effect of Org
33062 on glibenclamide pharmacodynamics and pharmacokinetics (and on glycemic
control), to investigate the effect of multiple doses of Org 33062 on steady state
pharmacokinetics of lithium, and to study the effect of multiple oral doses of Org 33062
on steady state pharmacodynamics of warfarin.
Glibenclamide (glyburide) – The Sponsor has evaluated the effect of Org 33062 on
insulin sensitivity in eighteen Type II diabetic patients before and after administration of
multiple doses of Org 33062 (40 mg daily). Insulin sensitivity index (mean glucose
infusion rate/((steady state insulin concentration)*(mean blood glucose)) was the
outcome measured in a test using the hyperinsulinemic, euglycemic clamp technique. No
statistically significant difference between Org 33062 and placebo were demonstrated. In
addition, in sixteen subjects with Type II diabetes taking individually adjusted
glibenclamide at an unchanged dose for at least 3 months, there was little change in
glucose tolerance (as measured by an oral glucose tolerance test) following 60day
administration of 40 mg daily Org 33062.
Glibenclamide pharmacokinetics were evaluated before and after administration of Org
33062 in the 16 patients who had the oral glucose tolerance test. There was an
approximate 20% decrease in Cmax and AUC for glibenclamide after exposure to Org
18

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
33062. In some cases the reduction was up to 50%, whereas some subjects had little
change. This resulted in a 90% confidence interval for the ratio of geometric means of
0.668-0.954. Since the 40 mg dose used in this study is considered to be a minimally
effective dose, and the dose could be titrated to 80 mg, the 40 mg dose used in this study
may not be clinically relevant, and the results cannot rule out an effect at the higher doses
that may be used clinically.
Lithium - The sponsor has evaluated the effect of multiple oral doses of Org 33062 (40
mg daily) on the steady state pharmacokinetics of lithium in twelve healthy male
subjects. In addition, the effect o multiple doses of lithium on steady state
pharmacokinetics was evaluated. Lithium was titrated over a 3-day period to a dose of
600 mg bid, and was maintained at that dose for 9 days. Org 40 mg was given on Days
7-10. There was a slight decrease in lithium Cmax (approximately 11%) in the presence
of Org 33062. After co-administration of lithium and Org- 33063, there was a slight
(<6%) decrease in Cmax and decrease in AUC for gepirone and a slight (<6%) increase
in AUC for 1-PP and for 3’-OH-geprione. This suggests no significant pharmacokinetic
interaction at a dose of Org 33062 40 mg daily co-administered with lithium. However,
as above, since the 40 mg dose of Org 33062 used in this study is considered to be a
minimally effective dose, and the dose could be titrated to 80 mg, the 40 mg dose used in
this study may not be clinically relevant, and the results cannot rule out a greater effect
on lithium pharmacokinetics at the higher doses that may be used clinically.
Warfarin - The Sponsor evaluated the effect of multiple oral doses of Org 33062 (40 mg
daily) on steady state pharmacodynamics of orally administered warfarin in sixteen
healthy male subjects. The outcome measure was the prothrombin time (PT),
standardized and expressed as the International Normalized Ratio (INR). The no effect
level (90% confidence interval) was considered by the Sponsor to be in the range of 0.91.11. The study showed that gepirone given at a dose of 40 mg daily had no effect on
anticoagulation in the presence of warfarin given 5 mg daily. Co-administration of
warfarin with Org 33062 caused a < 17% increase in exposure to gepirone and its
metabolites. The results do not support dose adjustment for either warfarin or Org 33062
during concomitant therapy. However, the dose of gepirone used was not the maximal
planned dose, and an effect at the 80 mg dose cannot be ruled out.

Were plasma concentrations of gepirone or any metabolites measured in the pivotal
efficacy trials?
The Sponsor has responded to the question above posed by OCPB in the nonapproval

concentrations were measured during all dosages of 20, 40, 60, and 80 mg in a total of
192 subjects. The findings were as follows:
• The dose normalized concentrations of each analyte were similar across the dose
levels (40-60 mg).

inverse correlation was found between plasma concentrations of 1-PP with
efficacy outcomes.

3.3
Recommendations
The Sponsor has provided studies or justification to address the recommendations of the
Office of Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics that were identified in the not
approvable letter. Although the Sponsor’s responses were generally acceptable, there are
several recently identified issues regarding the pharmacokinetics as well as recent
changes in the drug product that are of some concern.
•

The 20 and 80 mg strength tablets (with the modified flat tablet shape) are
acceptable, but the 40 and 80 mg strength tablets (with the modified flat tablet
shape) are not acceptable.
The nonequivalence of the 40 and 80 mg strength tablets compared to the
approved 20 mg strength of gepirone would most likely have little impact on
the adverse event profile. A biowaiver for the 60 mg strength as originally
manufactured could be granted. However, the Sponsor has now changed the
tablet shape with a change in commercial tooling from the original biconvex
tablet to the modified flat tablets. Dissolution has only been evaluated for the
20 mg and 80 mg strength tablets. Therefore the 40 and 60 mg strength
tablets (with the modified flat tablet shape) are not considered approvable at
this time.
• The Sponsor should submit results of dissolution comparisons (of
the original vs. modified shape) in multiple media with f2
comparisons to request a biowaiver for the 40 and 60 mg strength
tablets.

•

The Sponsor’s proposed dissolution specifications are acceptable on an interim
basis. The dissolution specifications are as follows:
15-25% at 1 h
40-85% at 5 h
65-86% at 12 h
> 86% at 20 h
These specifications are acceptable provided that the Sponsor adheres to the
stated commitment to re-evaluate the specification at 12 hours after the
manufacture of 20 batches of each strength.
1.2

•
•

Recommended Phase 4 Study Commitments

The effect of intermediate inducers such as rifabutin should be evaluated as a
Phase IV commitment.
The Sponsor has previously agreed to conduct an in vivo study evaluating the
effects of different meal compositions on gepirone ER pharmacokinetics as a
Phase IV commitment and states that the study has been initiated.

24

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
•

The Sponsor has stated a commitment to re-evaluate the specification at 12 hours
after the manufacture of 20 batches of each strength.

The proposed labeling is acceptable from the OCPB perspective provided that the
Sponsor make the recommended changes. Labeling comments can be found in section 4.
Comments to Medical Officer (do not send to Sponsor)
•

In the original submission, a significant pharmacokinetic interaction was observed
between ketoconazole, a potent inhibitor of CYP3A4, and gepirone (a 5-fold
increase in gepirone AUC). To minimize the risk of toxicity, we recommend that
coadministration with ketoconazole or other potent inhibitors of CYP3A4 be
considered as a warning.

•

A significant pharmacokinetic interaction was observed between gepirone and
rifampin, a potent inducer of CYP3A4 (a 20-fold decrease in Cmax and a 29-fold
decrease in AUC for gepirone). Because of the risk of reduced efficacy, we
recommend that coadministration with rifampin and other potent inducers should
be contraindicated.

•

A significant pharmacokinetic interaction was observed between gepirone and
verapamil, an intermediate inhibitor of CYP3A4 (a 2.6 fold increase in Cmax and
AUC was observed). To minimize the risk of toxicity, when verapamil and
gepirone are co-administered, gepirone should be used cautiously. A low dose of
gepirone is recommended with subsequent dosage adjustment based on clinical
assessment. We recommend that the dose of gepirone in these patients not exceed
40 mg/day.

•

As noted in the original submission, gepirone concentrations are increased 5080% in the elderly. However, exposure depends on other factors as well (such as
race for which there is a 1.5-2-fold decrease in AUC in Blacks and Asians
compared to Caucasians). To minimize the risk of toxicity in the elderly we
recommend that dosing in each patient should be individualized. Dosing of
(b) (4)
gepirone in the elderly should be started at the lowest strength and
.

•

In the original submission it was noted that plasma concentrations of gepirone and
its metabolites were elevated by approximately 70% in patients with moderate
and severe renal impairment. To minimize the risk of toxicity in patients with
moderate or severe renal impairment we recommend that dosing in each patient
should be individualized. Dosing of gepirone in patients with moderate or severe
(b) (4)
renal impairment should be started at the lowest strength and
.

•

In the original submission it was noted that gepirone exposure is augmented 2fold in patients with moderately impaired hepatic function. To minimize the risk

25

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
of toxicity, gepirone should be used cautiously in patients with moderately
impaired hepatic function. A low dose of gepirone is recommended with
subsequent dosage adjustment based on clinical assessment. We recommend that
the dose of gepirone in these patients not exceed 40 mg/day.
•

The pharmacokinetics of gepirone have not been adequately studied in patients
with severe hepatic impairment. Therefore we recommend that the drug not be
administered in this population and that there should be a warning or a
contraindication regarding use of gepirone in this population.

5.2

Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics Individual Study Review

5.2.1 Sponsor’s Approach to Justification for Approval of the 40 and 80 mg Strength
Tablets
Background
The sponsor requested approval of the 40, 60, and 80 mg Org 33062 ER tablets even
though the 40 mg and 80 mg tablets failed to meet bioequivalence to the 2 x 20 mg and 4
x 20 mg ER tablets. The sponsor claims that the increased plasma concentrations
observed from the 1 x 80 mg ER tablets are not clinically relevant. The sponsor’s claim
was based on the following evidence:
1. Plasma concentration-AE relationship in healthy subjects
2. Dose-AE relationship in patients with depression
Plasma concentration-AE relationship in healthy subjects
The sponsor conducted an analysis to determine the relationship between the exposure
parameters (Cmax, AUC) and the intensity of AE in healthy male subjects. Data for this
analysis came from studies 28717 and 28718.
Study 28717 was an open-label, randomized, single and multiple dose, three
treatment, two period crossover study in 25 healthy male subjects. Each subject
received a single 40 mg dose on day 1 followed by repeat dosing every 24 hours
until steady state concentrations were achieved (Treatment A). Each subject also
received multiple doses of 200 mg ketoconazole every 12 hours for 3 days
followed by a single dose of either 20 mg (treatment B) or 40 mg (treatment C)
gepirone.
Study 28718 was an open-label, randomized, single dose, four-way crossover
study in 32 healthy male subjects. Each subject received 1) 1 x 80 mg, 2) 4 x 20
mg, 3) 1 x 40 mg, and 4) 2 x 20 mg ER tablets in 4-way crossover design
separated by 7-day washout period.
To investigate the relationship between exposure parameters and AE intensity from these
studies, the sponsor compared the descriptive statistics of parameter values for subjects
who experienced dizziness, nausea, and vomiting to those subjects who did not have an
AE. Data was pooled by study day, i.e., single dose vs. steady state.
The results showed that for study 28717 there was a positive relationship between
exposure parameters and AE intensity for dizziness and nausea. A descriptive summary
for parameter values and AE intensity for dizziness is shown in Table 1.

101

NDA 21-164
Org 33062

1. The results of study 28718 showed lower Cmax and AUC values compared with

Dose-AE relationship in patients with depression
The sponsor conducted an analysis to investigate the relationship between dose and
adverse events. A logistic regression model was fit to AE data collected in 8 clinical
trials in patients with depression. The six adverse events included in the model were
dizziness, headache, insomnia, nausea, nervousness, and somnolence. The doses of
gepirone ranged from 20 to 80 mg across studies.
The final model was used to predict the increased risk for a given AE due to a 17%
increase in exposure when switching from 4 x 20 mg to 1 x 80 mg ER tablets. This was
accomplished by evaluating the risk of a particular AE for a 93.6 mg dose, which was
calculated as the “exposure equivalent” dose to the 1 x 80 mg dosage strength. The
logistic regression model predicted a 7% increase in dizziness and less than 3% increase
for insomnia and nausea.
Reviewer’s Comments
The reviewer concurs with the sponsor that the nonequivalence of the to-be-marketed
dosage strengths compared to the approved 20 mg dosage strength of gepirone will most
likely have little impact on the adverse event profile. Based on plasma concentration
observed in the phase 1 studies, the highest exposure to drug occurred in study 28717
during the concomitant administration of a single dose of 40 mg (2 x 20 mg) ER tablets
with 200 mg ketoconazole (Table 4). In that treatment group, 100 % of the subjects
experienced dizziness, 92% experienced nausea and 67% vomited. The Cmax values for
this treatment group ranged from 17.3–42.8 ng/ml. In comparison, co-administration of
20 mg gepirone with 200 mg ketoconazole resulted in greater than 50% of subjects
experiencing dizziness and nausea and 8% of subjects vomited.

Since gepirone will be used to treat a chronic condition, the difference between the two

single dose. Nonparametric superposition was used to simulate the mean steady-state
concentrations for the 1 x 80 mg and 4 x 20 mg ER tablets (Figure 2). Predicted steady
state concentrations for each formulation are very similar, with mean peak concentrations
of 11.1 ng/ml for the 1 x 80 mg tablets and 10.4 ng/ml for the 2 x 40 mg tablets. Total
exposures, as measured by AUC over the dosing interval, for each formulation are
153 ng.h/ml and 155 ng.h/ml, respectively.

OCPB concurs with the Sponsor that the nonequivalence of the to-be-marketed strengths
compared to the approved 20 mg strength of gepirone will most likely have little impact
on the adverse event profile. Thus 80 mg strength can be considered approvable. In
addition, since the 40 mg strength is bracketed by the 20 and 80 mg strengths, it can also
be considered approvable.

Dissolution profiles for test (60 mg) and reference (20 mg) tablets were compared using

compared to the 20 mg tablet. For each profile, the coefficient of variation was less than

vitro dissolution profiles in multiple media in comparison to the 20 mg strength tablet. In
addition, the 40 and 80 mg strength tablets showed similarity in all in vitro dissolution
conditions to the 20 mg strength tablets.

STUDY DESIGN:
This study was an open-label, randomized, 2-period, 2-treatment, 2-sequence crossover
study design with multiple doses of verapamil and single doses of Org 33062, as shown
in Table 2, below. For Sequence 1, subjects received a single oral dose of 20 mg Org
33062 ER on Day 1 (with blood samples drawn for 48 hours after dosing on Days 1-2).
Days 3-7 were a wash out period. Verapamil 80 mg tid was then dosed for 9 days (Day
8-16) and Org 33062 20 mg ER was co-administered on Day 15. For Sequence 2,
subjects received verapamil 80 mg tid for 9 days (starting at Day -7) and Org 33062 ER
was co-administered on Day 1 (with blood samples drawn from 48 hours after dosing on

111

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
Days 1-2). After a 12-day washout period (Days 3-14), Org 33062 20 mg ER was

Inclusion criteria included healthy male Caucasian subjects 18-45 y.o. Subjects were to
smoke < 10 cigarettes/day and be able to refrain from smoking during the in-house
period. Subjects were to refrain from use of methylxanthines from 48 hours prior to the
first dose to after the last pharmacokinetic sample, and refrain from grapefruit-containing
products from 7 days before the 1st dose to after the last pharmacokinetic sample.
Exclusion criteria included the use of any drug or substance known to induce drug
metabolizing enzymes within 1 month prior to the 1st dose, and any substance known to
inhibit drug metabolizing enzymes or any drug within 1 week prior to the 1st dose (except
for acetaminophen).
Subjects were fasted from 10 hours prior to the dose of Org 33062 until 4 hours after
dosing. Blood samples were collected at fixed times and plasma stored at –20° C. In
each sequence blood samples were collected for determination of gepirone, 3′-OHgepirone, and 1-PP pre-dose and at 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 12, 18, 24, 30, 36, and 48 hours
after dosing of Org 33062. Twenty-four hour urine collection was performed on Days
1 and 15 of each Sequence for determination of 6β-hydroxycortisol and free cortisol.

These results show a greater than two-fold increase in systemic exposure to gepirone
(approximately 163% increase in Cmax and in AUC), with an approximate 65% decrease
in apparent oral clearance following co-administration of gepirone with verapamil. A
shortening of the tmax was also observed. There was little change in exposure to either
of the metabolites.
Safety
Fourteen of 31 subjects eligible for the safety analysis reported adverse events. The most
prominent adverse events were dizziness and headache. The majority of adverse events
were reported during the verapamil dosing phase with headache and dizziness most
frequently observed. Headache and dizziness were also reported after Org 33062 alone,
and these were the only adverse effects reported with Org 33062 alone. Incidence of
headache increased when Org 33062 was given with verapamil, compared to when Org
33062 was given alone. Adverse events of toothache, upper respiratory tract infection,
and increased sweating were not considered to be drug-related. The Sponsor states that
there were no clinically significant abnormal laboratory values, vital signs, or changes in
ECG. The Sponsor states that gepirone given alone or with verapamil was well tolerated
in this study.
CONCLUSIONS:
This study demonstrated a significant pharmacokinetic interaction between gepirone and
verapamil, resulting in significantly increased exposure (163%) to gepirone.

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
5.2.4

December 2001)

STUDY DESIGN:
This study was an open-label, randomized, multiple dose, 2-period, 2-treatment, 2sequence crossover study design with multiple doses of rifampicin and multiple doses of
Org 33062, as shown in Table 1, below. There was a washout of at least 4 weeks
between the 2 treatment periods.

NDA 21-164

Inclusion criteria included healthy male or female subjects 18-45 y.o. Subjects were to
be nonsmokers for at least 3 months. Subjects were to be extensive metabolizers of
CYP2D6 (as determined phenotyping with sparteine at least one week prior to the first
dose). Exclusion criteria included medication or drug use of any kind within 2 weeks
of the first dosing (except for acetaminophen and sparteine (that was used for
phenotyping)) or within 1 month prior to the first dosing with any agent known to induce
or inhibit drug metabolizing enzymes (e.g. rifampicin, cimetidine, ketoconazole,
phenytoin, macrolides, and compounds in the barbiturate and phenothiazine classes).
Consumption of alcohol, caffeine, and other methylxanthines was not allowed from 48
hours prior to the first dosing on day 1 until the last pharmacokinetic blood sampling
during each study period. Consumption of St. John’s wort and grapefruit containing
products was not allowed from 10 days prior to the first dosing on Day 1 until the last
pharmacokinetic blood sampling during each study period.
Subjects were dosed between 8AM and 10AM each morning, without having had
breakfast. On Day 7, the dose was administered after a 10 hour overnight fast, with
fasting continued until at least 4 hours after dosing (except for a light snack of toast with
butter/marmalade and orange juice) at 2 hours post dose). Blood samples were collected
at fixed times and plasma stored at –20° C. Blood samples were collected for
determination of gepirone, 3′-OH-gepirone, and 1-PP pre-dose on days 1, 5, 6, and 7 and
at 0.5, 1, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 10, 12, 18, 24, 30, 36, and 48 hours after dosing on day 7.
Twenty-four hour urine collection was performed on Days -1 and 7 of each trial period
and stored at -20° C for determination of 6β-hydroxycortisol and free cortisol.

One calibration curve and duplicate QC samples were analyzed with each batch of study

values for the experimental samples fell within the low and medium QC samples, the
Sponsor considered this run to be acceptable.
There was no information provided on the stability of the samples. The samples were
analyzed within 6 months of beginning the clinical study.
RESULTS:
Demographics
Twenty-seven subjects (22 male and 5 female) were enrolled in the study. Three male
subjects discontinued and were replaced. Twenty-four subjects completed the study and
were eligible for pharmacokinetic analysis. Of the three subjects who withdrew from the
study, 1 subject discontinued due to adverse effects (dizziness, headache, and
disorientation) on day 4 of study period 1 (treatment A), 1 subject was withdrawn due to
a protocol violation, and 1 subject discontinued after completion of trial period 1
(treatment B) due to abnormal high laboratory values (AST and ALT) that subsequently
returned to normal ranges. The mean age of subjects enrolled in the study was 33 years
of age, and the range was 22 to 45 years old. Demographics of the subjects completing
the study are shown below.

These results show a decrease in exposure to gepirone of approximately 20-fold and 29fold as determined by Cmax and AUC, respectively, after exposure to rifampin, with an
approximate 29-fold increase in apparent oral clearance. There were also decreases in
exposure observed for the 3’-OH gepirone metabolite (an approximate 2.5-fold decrease
in Cmax, a 2.9-fold decrease in AUC, and a 2.9-fold increase in apparent oral clearance),
but much smaller changes were observed for the 1-PP metabolite.
The ratio of 6β-hydroxycoritsol to free cortisol in urine was used as a marker for
CYP3A4 activity, in which a higher ratio would indicate higher CYP3A4 activity. For
this evaluation there were 20 evaluable subjects with complete data. The results in the
evaluable subjects are shown below.

For Gepirone alone, the point estimate for the ratio of post/pre-treatment (Day 7/8: Day 1/1) is 1.03 (90% confidence interval 0.85-1.26). For gepirone + rifampin the point
estimate for the ratio of post/pre-treatment is 4.62 (90% confidence interval 3.83-5.57).
These results confirm that induction occurred in the presence of rifampin, and suggest
that gepirone alone did not induce CYP3A4.
Safety
All but one subject eligible for safety evaluation (26 subjects who received treatment A
and 25 subjects who received treatment B) experienced at least one adverse event after
administration of Org 33062 alone or concomitant with rifampicin. This included 88.5%
of subjects who received treatment A and 76% of subjects who received treatment B.
One subject experienced severe dizziness on Day 4 of period 1 (Treatment A). This
subject discontinued due to severe dizziness, mild headache, and mild disorientation.
The most prominent adverse events were headache, dizziness, fatigue, diarrhea, and
nausea in both reference and test groups. Headache and dizziness were more often
reported during treatment with Org 33062 alone, while diarrhea was reported more often
with concomitant rifampicin. The dizziness reported with Org 33062 alone was of
greater intensity than that with Org 30062 given with rifampicin. One subject had
significantly high values of AST and ALT on day 8 of treatment B that returned to the
normal range after discontinuation.

CONCLUSIONS:
This study demonstrated a significant pharmacokinetic interaction between gepirone and
rifampicin, resulting in significantly reduced exposure to gepirone (more than 20-fold)
and its 3’-OH metabolite (more than 2-fold).

5.2.5

Paroxetine Interaction Study

A DOUBLE-BLIND, RANDOMIZED MULTIPLE ORAL DOSE, THREE-WAY
CROSS-OVER STUDY TO ASSESS THE PHARMACOKINETIC
INTERACTION OF ORG 33062 AND PAROXETINE IN HEALTHY
VOLUNTEERS
Study Investigators and Site:
L. Tritsmans, MD
C&T Paradigm NV
Antwerp, Belgium
Protocol Number: 28703

OBJECTIVES:
The primary objective was to assess the effect of multiple oral dosing of paroxetine on
the steady state pharmacokinetics of Org 33062 and vice versa. Secondary objectives
were to assess the effect of multiple oral dosing of paroxetine on the pharmacodynamics
of Org 33062 and vice versa, and to assess the safety and tolerability of multiple oral
doses of paroxetine and Org 33062, given separately and in combination.
FORMULATIONS:

STUDY DESIGN:
This study was double-blind, multiple oral dose, randomized, three-period cross-over
pharmacokinetic interaction trial of Org 33062 and paroxetine. The treatments were as
follows:
Treatment A: oral administration of 20 mg Org 33062 once daily on Days 1 and 2,
40 mg Org 33062 once daily on Days 3 to 8 and paroxetine placebo Days
1-9

124

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
Treatment B: oral administration of 20 mg paroxetine once daily on Days 1-9
Org 33062 placebo on Days 1-8
Treatment C: oral administration of 20 mg Org 33062 once daily on Days 1 and 2,
40 mg Org 33062 once daily on Days 3-8 and 20 mg paroxetine once daily on Days 1-9.
The design is shown in Table 1 below (as provided by Sponsor).
of at least 2 weeks between successive treatment periods.

There was a washout

Inclusion criteria included healthy male or female subjects 18-45 y.o. Subjects were to
be nonsmokers for at least 3 months. Subjects were to be extensive metabolizers of
CYP2D6 (as determined by phenotyping with dextromethorphan at least one week prior
to the first dose). Exclusion criteria included medication or drug use of any kind within
2 weeks of the first dosing (except for acetaminophen and dextromethorphan (for
phenotyping)) or within 1 month prior to the first dosing with any agent known to induce
or inhibit drug metabolizing enzymes (e.g. rifampicin, cimetidine, ketoconazole,
phenytoin, macrolides, and compounds in the barbiturate and phenothiazine classes).
Consumption of alcohol, grapefruit-containing products, and caffeine or other
methylxanthines was not allowed from 48 hours prior to the first dosing on Day 1 until
the last pharmacokinetic blood sampling in each trial period.
Subjects were dosed between 8AM and 10AM each morning. Breakfast was eaten
between 0.5 and 1 hour after dosing, except on Day 8 of each period in which the dose
was administered after a 10-hour overnight fast and fasting continued until at least 4
hours after dosing (except for a snack at 2 hours after dosing). Blood samples were
collected at fixed times and plasma stored at –20° C. Blood samples were collected for
determination of gepirone, 3′-OH-gepirone, and 1-PP as well as paroxetine at pre-dose
on days 1, 6, and 7, as well as at pre-dose, 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 10, 12, 18, 24, 30, 36,
and 48 hours after dosing on Day 8 in each period. Psychopharmacologic effects were to
be studied using a visual analogue rating scale that included the following items:
alert/drowsy, calm/excited, strong/feeble, muzzy/clear headed, well-coordinated/clumsy,
lethargic/energetic, contented/discontented, trouble/tranquil, mentally slow/quick witted,
tense/relaxed, attentive/dreamy, incompetent/proficient, happy/sad,
antagonistic/amicable, interested/bored, withdrawn/gregarious. The Visual Analogue
Scales were to be completed in each period on Days 1 and 8 at 0, 1, 3, 5, and 8 hours
after dosing.
125

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
Phenotyping for CYP2D6 was performed at screening more than 1 week prior to the first
dose of trial medication in Period 1 and was repeated in the mornings of Day 7 of each
period. Subjects were given 30 mg dextromethorphan hydrobromide (Touxium) with 200
ml of water after emptying their bladder for phenotyping for CYP2D6. Urine samples
were collected from 0-8 hours post-dose. Urine aliquots were frozen and stored at -86° C
until assayed.

These results show no significant impact of paroxetine on exposure to gepirone or 3’OHgepirone. However, an approximate 2.6-fold (155%) increase in Cmax and an
approximate 3.12-fold (195%) increase in the 1-PP metabolite is observed, with an
approximate 3-fold (69%) decrease in apparent oral clearance and an approximate 4-fold
(270%) increase in Cminavg for that metabolite. It is also noted that the mean t1/2 for
gepirone, 1-PP, and Org 25907 were prolonged 1.6, 1.9, and 1.4 times, respectively after
exposure to paroxetine.
No significant impact of gepirone on exposure to paroxetine is observed.
Effect of Treatment on CYP2D6 Phenotype
The Sponsor evaluated the effect of each treatment on CYP2D6 phenotype (assessed by
dextromethorphan/dextrorphan urinary ratio). The results are shown in the table below,
as provided by the Sponsor.

The ratios were higher (2.7, 86, and 123 times) for Org 33062 alone, paroxetine alone,
and combination, respectively, compared to pre-treatment screening, and the effect was
statistically significant. However, it should be noted that exposure to the 40 mg Org
33062 was not sufficient to convert extensive metabolizers to phenotypic poor
133

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
metabolizers. There was no statistically significant difference in the ratios following
paroxetine alone treatment and the combination treatment. This data supports the
inhibitory effect of paroxetine on CYP2D6.
Pharmacodynamics
There were no statistically significant effects and no consistent effects between
treatments on the Visual Analogue Scales.
Safety
There were no deaths or serious adverse events in this trial, and none of the subjects
discontinued the trial due to adverse events. Two subjects temporarily interrupted
treatment due to adverse events in period one that included nausea and dizziness. One or
more adverse events occurred in a total of 29 subjects. There were 321 adverse events
reported, and 46.4% were reported during the first treatment period, mostly during the
first 3 treatment days. The most frequently reported adverse events were dizziness,
headache, nausea, somnolence, and fatigue. Dizziness was mostly reported in the Org
33062 treatment period. Systolic and diastolic blood pressures were lower during
treatment with Org 33062 than during paroxetine or combination treatment. Seven
subjects had abnormally low vital signs values during treatment with Org 33062 and/or
combination treatment. One subject had syncope during treatment with Org 33062 and
one subject had syncope during combination treatment. One other subject had orthostatic
dizziness during treatment with Org 33062.

CONCLUSIONS:
Data from this study suggest that paroxetine alters the disposition of gepirone by
increasing exposure to the 1-PP metabolite, but not to the parent compound or to the 3’OH metabolite. The clinical relevance of this is unknown.
This study indicates that gepirone at a 40 mg dose does not inhibit the metabolism of
paroxetine, a CYP2D6 substrate. The results (no inhibition) are consistent with in vitro
studies showing no inhibition of CYP2D6 by gepirone, and suggest that gepirone will not
alter the metabolism of drugs metabolized by CYP2D6 when used at this dose.
However, the dose of Org 33062 selected was not optimal for showing an inhibitory
effect on CYP2D6 as this is not the maximum planned dose.

NDA 21-164

5.2.6. Glyburide Interaction Study (Study 28707)
THE EFFECT OF ORG 33062 ON INSULIN SENSITIVITY AND
GLIBENCLAMIDE PHARMACOKIENTICS AND PHARMACODYNAMICS
Study Investigators and Site:
A. Schrödter, MD, PhD
FOCUS Clinical Drug Development, GmbH
Neuss, Germany
Protocol Number: 28707

OBJECTIVES:
The objective of Trial block I was to investigate the effect of Org 33062 on insulin
sensitivity in type II diabetic patients.
The objectives of Trial block II were 1) to investigate the effect of Org 33062 on
glibenclamide pharmacokinetics in type II diabetic patients and 2) to investigate the
effect of Org 33062 on glibenclamide pharmacodynamics in type II diabetic patients.
(Note: glibenclamide is the same drug as glyburide, the name used in the United States).
FORMULATIONS:
Table 1. Products used in 28707

Org 33062 20 mg extended
release tablets (
)

Batch Lot
Number
CP CV053
(b) (4)

Expiration Date
(Dates of Study)
03/2004
(August, 2001 –
June 2002)

STUDY DESIGN:
Block I was a randomized, double-blind, placebo controlled multiple dose trial in obese
Type II diabetic patients. Each subject received only 1 treatment (9 subjects received
Org 33062 and 9 subjects received placebo). Following a washout period of 3 weeks
during which subjects were to discontinue their current drug therapy, subjects received
placebo or Org 33062 for 3 weeks. The dose regimen for Org 33062 was 20 mg once
daily for 2 days and then 40 mg (2x20 mg) taken once daily 1 hour before breakfast.
In Block 1, insulin-mediated glucose disposal was to be measured using a two-step
hyperinsulinemic, euglycemic clamp technique as follows. Blood glucose concentrations
were clamped at 110 mg/dl (6.1 mmol/L). The blood glucose concentration had to be
established for at least 32 hours before the insulin infusion rate was changed. At time 0,
the insulin infusion rate was 0.5 mU/kg/min for 2 hours after which it was changed to 1.5

135

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
mU/kg/min for 2 hours. The last 30 minutes of the 2 insulin infusion rate phases were to
be regarded as steady state phases. After an initial baseline clamp, subjects began the
treatment period. Subjects reported to the clinic on days 7 and 14 for assessment of
fasted blood glucose. A second clamp was performed on day 21. Compliance of
treatment and diet were ascertained by interview, measurement of body weight, pill
count, and trough concentrations of Org 33062.
Block II was an open, one sequence crossover, non-controlled study in Type II diabetic
subjects with an individually adjusted anti-diabetic treatment with glibenclamide
unchanged for at least 3 months. Org 33062 (2x20 mg) was to be co-administered once
daily 1 hour before breakfast for 6 days. Pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic
assessments (oral glucose tolerance test) were to be performed before Org 33062 dosing
and at the sixth day of Org 33062 co-administration.
Inclusion criteria for both study blocks included male or female subjects between 18 and
70 years of age. All physical examination parameters were to be without signs of
clinically relevant pathology and all values for hematology and clinical chemistry within
the normal range or showing no clinically relevant deviations (except for triglycerides
and cholesterol). Women must have been post-menopausal, surgically sterile, or practice
an acceptable method of contraception. In addition, for Block I, subjects were to be
obese Type II diabetes mellitus patients for at least 3 months duration, controlled with
diet alone and/or on oral hypoglycemics, with fasting blood sugar greater than 7.0
mmol/L. Subjects for Block II must have been Type II diabetic patients treated with
glibenclamide for at least 3 months before the study and have a fasting blood sugar
greater than 7.0 mmol/L and HbA1c up to 8%. Exclusion criteria included history of
liver disease or abnormal renal function. Subjects were not allowed to use concomitant
medication within 21 days prior to the first administration of trial medication until
follow-up except for sporadic use3 of acetaminophen. However, subjects were allowed
to maintain their usual antihypertensive or contraceptive medication or hormone replace
therapy, and for block II glibenclamide was permitted.
In Block 1, blood samples were collected for determination of Org 33062 trough
concentrations on days -1, 7, 14, and 21 prior to the daily dose. In Block 2, pre-dose
blood samples for determination of Org 33062 trough concentrations were collected on
days 1-6. Blood samples for determination of glibenclamide plasma concentrations were
collected on day -1 and on day 6. On both days samples were collected at pre-dose and at
0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, and 24 hours after dosing. Plasma was stored at -18º C
until analysis.

Pharmacokinetics

In Blocks I and II, the Sponsor obtained trough concentrations of Org 33062 on Day 7,
14, and 21 to determine compliance with Org 33062 administration. In addition, the
reviewer has reviewed the slopes of the trough concentrations for individual subjects that
suggest steady state had been reached (with the exception of 1 individual in block 2 for
whom plasma concentrations continued to increase on each day).
Glibenclamide Pharmacokinetics in Block II
Individual plasma concentration-time profiles for glibenclamide before and after
administration of Org 33062 were shown by the Sponsor. However, since each patient
had an individually adjusted dosage regimen, the mean curves were not presented by the
Sponsor.
Pharmacokinetic parameters for glibenclamide were determined using noncompartmental
analysis. The pertinent pharmacokinetic parameters are shown in the Tables 5 and 6,
below. Two subjects had unusually high pre-dose glibenclamide values on Day -1
compared to pre-dose values on Day 6 , suggesting that they had taken a dose on the
evening prior to dosing on Day -1. A separate analysis excluded these patients, with
pharmacokinetic results similar to that of the whole group. The results of the whole

Pharmacodynamics
In Block I, the main objective was to show that the change of insulin sensitivity index to
the baseline value is higher for treatment with Org 33062 than for treatment with placebo
(using an unpaired t-test or non-parametric Wilcoxon test if the distribution was not
normal. Insulin sensitivity index was defined as follows where GIR is mean glucose
infusion rate, Ins is steady state insulin concentration and BG is mean blood glucose:

The Table below (as provided by Sponsor) shows mean values for changes from baseline
for SI as well as for GIR and other parameters as indicated. No statistically significant
differences between Org 33062 and placebo were demonstrated (either by t-test or
Wilcoxon).

139

NDA 21-164
Org 33062

In Block II an oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT) was performed on Day -1, immediately
after glibenclamide administration and on Day 6, immediately after administration of Org
33062. Food intake was standardized and patients were not allowed to exert unusual
physical activity on the 2 days preceding the test and during the days of the
measurements. On the days of the OGTT, following overnight fast, patients drank a
solution of 75 g glucose in 300 ml over not more than 5 minutes. The test was performed
at the same time of day for each subject. Blood samples were collected after glucose
administration for measurement of glucose and insulin. Summary statistics for AUC0-4,
Cmax and Tmax for glucose and insulin, as well as a comparison between Day 6
(Gepirone + Glibenclamide) and Day -1 (Glibenclamide), are shown in the Tables below,
as provided by the Sponsor. (The AUC0-6 has not been considered by the reviewer since
this data was missing in 5/16 subjects).

In addition, the reviewer has evaluated the results at 2 hours, the standard time for
evaluation of the OGTT. The ratio of the geometric means (Test Day 6/Ref Day-1) was
1.01 for glucose and 1.03 for insulin, with 90% confidence intervals of 0.78-1.30 and
0.66-1.59, respectively. Thus following a 6-day administration of 40 mg daily Org
33062, there was little change in glucose tolerance.
Safety
In trial Block I there were 4 subjects dosed with Org 33062 and 4 subjects dosed with
placebo that reported possible drug-related adverse events. These included dizziness,
headache, paresthesia, abdominal pain, diarrhea, nervousness, and sleep disorder for Org
33062 and headache, diarrhea, impotence, insomnia, and somnolence for placebo. Most
adverse events were judged as mild in severity. Two subjects on Org 33062 and two on
placebo reported moderate adverse events. In one subject the maximum intensity (of
dizziness) was severe).
In trial Block II there were 11 reports of baseline adverse events in 8 subjects, including 3
subjects with mild to moderate hypoglycemia during the oral glucose tolerance test on
day -1, and 1 subject with hypoglycemic shock. All of those subjects recovered after
intake of dextrose. There were 32 adverse events in trial block 2, of which 24 were
141

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
assessed as possibly drug related. These included dizziness, fatigue, and insomnia, as
well as headache, tremor, asthenia, cold clammy skin, and sweating. There were no
abnormalities in laboratory values, ECG or vital signs that were considered by the
investigator or Sponsor to be clinically relevant.
CONCLUSIONS:
This study did not result in overall significant changes in insulin sensitivity in Type II
diabetes mellitus patients or in glibenclamide pharmacodynamics (as determined by
OGTT) in Type II diabetes mellitus patients. There were statistically significant changes
in glibenclamide Cmax and AUC (mean approximately 20%) with up to a 50% reduction
in some cases. However, since the 40 mg dose used in this study is considered to be a
minimally effective dose, and the dose could be titrated clinically to 80 mg, the 40 mg
dose used in this study may not be clinically relevant. Therefore, the results of this study
cannot rule out a greater effect at the higher doses that may be used clinically.

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
5.2.7 Lithium Interaction Study
A double-blind, randomized, two way crossover study to investigate the steady state
pharmacokinetics and tolerability of Org 33062 and lithium, given alone and coadministered, in healthy, young male subjects
Study Investigators and Site:
J-P Decourt, MD
Parexel-Cemef
6, Avenue Mozart

Protocol Number: 28706

OBJECTIVES:
The primary objective was to study the effect of multiple oral doses of Org 33602 on
steady state pharmacokinetics of lithium.
Secondary objectives were to investigate the effect of multiple doses of Lithium on
steady state pharmacokinetics of Org 33062, to investigate safety and tolerability of
multiple doses of Org 33062 and lithium, to explore the effects of Org 33062 on Lithium
pharmacodynamics, and to explore the effect of Lithium on Org 33062
pharmacodynamics.

STUDY DESIGN:
The study was a double-blind (for lithium), randomized, two-period, crossover design in
12 healthy male subjects. Each subject was randomized to a two-period, treatment
sequence with a one-week washout period between periods (Table 2).
