Subjects were included if they were healthy, Caucasian males between the ages of 18 and
45 years, and having a body mass index within 18 to 28 kg/m2. Exclusion criteria
included the use of any drug or substance known to induce of inhibit drug metabolizing
enzymes within one month prior to the first dose; and any drug within two weeks of
dosing (except for acetaminophen and dextromethorphan). Furthermore, subjects were
excluded if they had sub-clinical hypothyroidism, low serum-sodium concentrations, and
smoked more than 5 cigarettes per day.
Lithium was titrated over a three day period; each subject received 300 mg bid on Day 1,
450 mg bid on Day 2, and 600 mg bid on Day 3. The investigator was able to adjust the
lithium dose in individuals who had serum concentrations outside the safety range of 0.5–
0.8 mmol/L. Subjects were maintained on 600 mg bid for 9 more days. Blood samples
were collected prior to the morning dose (trough concentration) on Days 2 to Day 12 to
assess safety. In addition, blood samples were collected on Day 6 and Day 10 at 1, 2, 3,
4, 6, 8, 10, and 12 hours after the morning dose to assess lithium pharmacokinetics in the
absence and presence, respectively, of steady state concentrations of gepirone and its
metabolites.
Subjects received a 40 mg dose of Org 33062 once daily on Day 7 to Day 10. Blood
samples for determination of Org 33062, 1-PP, and 3’-OH plasma concentrations were
taken pre-dose and 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 12, 18, 24, 30, 36, 48, 60 and 72 hours after
administration of Org 3308 on Day 10. An additional blood sample was taken pre-dose
on Day 9 for determination of the attainment of steady state. Urine collection was not
performed.
Pharmacodynamic effects were evaluated using visual analogue scales for mood and
sleep on Days 6, 7, and 10.

validated methodologies. The bioassay for lithium was developed, validated, and

Statistical Testing for Drug Interaction
The results of statistical testing for drug interaction testing for lithium, as well as for
gepirone and its metabolites are shown in Table 7. The sponsor concluded the absence of
a drug interaction if the 90% confidence interval for geometric mean ratio of
(test/reference) was contained within the no effect range of 0.80–1.25. The test treatment
was concomitant administration of Org 33062 with lithium and the reference treatment
was administration of either Org 33062 or lithium alone.

Pharmacodynamics
No effect on mood or sleep scale parameters was noted.
Safety
Nine of 12 subjects reported a total of 36 adverse events (21 were mild and 15 were
moderate in intensity). The most common AE was nausea (reported in 6/12 subjects)
followed by dizziness (reported in 5/12 subjects) and diarrhea (reported in 4/12 subjects).
Subjects presented normal laboratory values (hematology, biochemistry, and urinalysis)
during the study. Values outside the normal range were judged by the investigator to be
not clinically significant. One subject, however, experienced a transient increase in
ALAT concentration that resolved without further incidence.
There was no influence of study treatment on vital sign parameters, ECG, and physical
examination.

149

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
Conclusions
This study showed there is no significant pharmacokinetic interaction between lithium
and Org 33062 at a gepirone dose of 40 mg daily coadministered for 3 days with lithium.
Since the 40 mg dose used in this study is not the maximally planned dose, an effect of
the 80 mg dose cannot be ruled out.

Warfarin Interaction Study

A trial to study the effect of multiple oral doses Org 33062 on steady state
pharmacodynamics of orally administered warfarin in healthy male volunteers
Study Investigators and Site:
KM. Eckl, MD
Pharm PlanNet Contract Research GmbH
Hindenburgstr. 306
D-41061 Monchengladbach, Germany
Protocol Number: 28704

OBJECTIVES:
The primary objective was to study the effect of multiple oral doses of Org 33602 on
steady state pharmacodynamics of orally administered warfarin in healthy volunteers.
Secondary objectives were 1) to study the effect of multiple oral doses of warfarin on
single dose pharmacokinetics of Org 33062 and 2) to study the safety and tolerability of
the combined use of warfarin and Org 33062.

STUDY DESIGN:
The study was a multiple dose, double-blind (for gepirone), placebo controlled,
randomized, two-sequence, two-period, crossover study to evaluate the effects of Org
33062 on steady state warfarin pharmacodynamics. Sixteen healthy male volunteers
were randomized to a treatment sequence (Table 2).

151

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
Table 2. Treatment Sequence and Dosing Schedule in 28704

(Days 18 to 23)

Subjects were included if they were healthy, nonsmoking, Caucasian males between the
ages of 18 and 45 years, and having a body mass index within 19 to 29 kg/m2. Exclusion
criteria included the use of any drug or substance known to induce of inhibit drug
metabolizing enzymes within one month prior to the first dose; any drug or vitamin
within one week of dosing (except for acetaminophen); and vitamin K containing
preparations.
Warfarin was initiated with a loading dose (20/15/5 mg/day) for 3 days to obtain steady
state prothrombin time by Day 4. This dosing regimen aimed to have subjects within a
sub-therapeutic anticoagulation range of 1.4 – 2.0 International Normalized Ratio (INR).
Subjects were maintained on 5 mg/d until Day 23. Blood samples were collected on
Days 5–6, 11–12, and 22–23 for determination of prothrombin time.
Subjects received a single 40 mg dose of Org 33062 on Day 1, followed by multiple daily
doses of 40 mg on Days 7-12 in Period 1 (Sequence 1) and Days 18-23 in Period 2
(Sequence 2). Blood samples for determination of Org 33062, 1-PP, and 3’-OH were
taken pre-dose and 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 12, 18, and 24 hours after administration of Org
3308 on Days 1, 7 (period 1), and 18 (period 2). Furthermore, blood samples were taken
at pre-dose on Days 8–11 (period 1) and Days 19–22 (period 2) for determination of the
attainment of steady state. Urine collection was not performed.

These results show that the 90% confidence interval for the geometric mean ratio of
Cmax for gepirone and 3’-OH gepirone fall within the no effect range of 0.8 to 1.25. For
the metabolite 1-PP, the geometric mean for Cmax was approximately 12% higher in the
presence of warfarin. The 90% confidence interval for the geometric mean ratio fell
outside the no effect range, indicating that the absence of a drug interaction could not be
shown.
For AUC for gepirone and both metabolites, absence of a drug interaction could not be
shown as the 90% confidence interval for gepirone and its metabolites was outside the no
effect interval of 0.80 to 1.25.
Safety:
Eleven of 16 subjects reported adverse events. Dizziness was the most common AE
reported following administration of Org 33062. Five subjects reported mild dizziness
following single dose administration of Org 33062 on Day 1. Eight subjects reported
157

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
dizziness (7 were mild and 1 moderate) following the co-administration of Org 33062
with warfarin.
Compared to baseline, laboratory values for prothrombin time and factors II, VII, and IX
were decreased and the parameters for aPTT and PT (INR) were increased during
warfarin treatment.
There was no influence of Org 33062 on vital sign parameters, ECG, and physical
examination.
Conclusions:
This study showed that gepirone given at a dose of 40 mg daily had no effect on
anticoagulation in the presence of warfarin given 5 mg daily. Co-administration of
warfarin with Org 33062 caused < 17% increase in exposure to gepirone and its
metabolites. The treatment regimens were safe and well-tolerated.
In the opinion of the reviewer, the results of this study do not support dose adjustment for
either warfarin or Org 33062 during concomitant therapy of Org 33062 and warfarin.
However, the dose of gepirone used was not the maximal planned dose and an effect at
the 80 mg dose cannot be ruled out.

5.2.8

Organon Bioanalytical Method for Gepirone, Org 25907, and 1-PP in Plasma

The following method was performed for samples from Study 28724, 28707, and 28705.
Analyses of plasma concentrations of gepirone and Org 25907 (3’-OH-gepirone) and its
metabolite 1-PP were performed using and electrospray (ESP)-HPLC/MS (LC/MS)
procedure in multi reaction monitoring mode. The methods were developed and
performed at Organon Development GmbH, Waltrop, Germany. (Organon R&D RR
report NL0031235 for Org 33062 and Org 25907 and report NL0030691 for 1-PP).
(b) (4)

For gepirone and Org 25907, aliquots of plasma are spiked with
as internal
standard. Saturated sodium borate solution is added to the samples, and the samples are
then extracted with (2:1) n-hexane:chloroform. The organic layer is evaporated to
dryness and the residue is reconstituted in 100 µl of 50:50 acetonitrile/0.05 M ammonium
acetate solution, pH 4.2. An aliquot is injected into the LC, and the analysis is
performed using LC-MS/MS.
(b) (4)

For 1-PP, aliquots of plasma are spiked with
as internal standard.
Saturated sodium borate solution is added to the samples, and the samples are then
extracted with (2:1) n-hexane:chloroform. The organic layer is extracted with 1: formic
acid solution. The organic layer is discarded and the analysis is performed on the 1%
formic acid layer. An aliquot is injected into the LC, and the analysis is performed using
LC-MS/MS.
Standard operating procedures (SOPs) were in place for sample preparation, the
analytical procedure, acceptance of the bioanalytical run (system suitability and
acceptance of calibration standards and quality control (QC) samples).
Selectivity, Accuracy, Precision, and Recovery
Selectivity was determined by analysis of blank samples from 10 different individuals
with respect to gepirone, Org 25907, 1-PP, and internal standard. Ranges of the
calibration curves, LOQ for each analyte, and nominal values for the QC samples are
shown in Table 1 below.

A calibration curve consisted of 8 non-zero standards run in duplicate. Also included are
a blank and a zero sample (plasma with internal standard) for each analytical run. Seven
sets of calibration curves were performed for gepirone and Org 25907, and six series
were performed for 1-PP. Linearity was established (mean r > .994), using linear
weighted (1/conc2) regression. The accuracy (calculated by the reviewer) and precision
for each nonzero standard ranged from -12.0-10.2% and 3.7-7.4%, respectively, for
gepirone and from -3.6-4.0% and from 2.9-6.2%, respectively, for Org 25907, and are
therefore acceptable. For 1-PP the accuracy ranged from -4.0-3.5% and the precision
ranged from 3.4-6.2%. These values are acceptable.
For gepirone and for Org 25907, accuracy and precision were analyzed on one assay day
for 6 replicates of each of the 5 quality control (QC) samples listed above for each
analyte. The 400 ng/ml sample was diluted 10-fold with blank human plasma prior to
analysis. The intra-day accuracy and precision ranged from -15.0-2.7% and from 4.79.9%, respectively for gepirone and from -14.2-8.5% and from 4.6-8.6%, respectively for
Org 25907. Seven sets of QC samples run in triplicate (in three series there were 6

160

NDA 21-164
Org 33062
replicates) were used to determine inter-day accuracy and precision. Accuracy and
precision ranged from -12.3-6.3% and 2.8-6.7%, respectively for gepirone except at the
LOQ where accuracy was 5% and precision was 20%. For Org 25907, accuracy and
precision ranged from -9.0-4.5% and from 4.5-9.6%, respectively. These values are
acceptable.
For 1-PP, accuracy and precision were analyzed on one assay day for 6 replicates of each
of the 5 quality control (QC) samples listed above. The 400 ng/ml sample was diluted
10-fold with blank human plasma prior to analysis. The intra-day accuracy and precision
ranged from -2.6-7.5% and from 2.7-5.3%, respectively. Six sets of QC samples run in
triplicate (in three series there were 6 replicates) were used to determine inter-day
accuracy and precision. Accuracy and precision ranged from 0.8-15% and 1.3-10.7%,
respectively. These values are acceptable.

Stability
Stability of gepirone, Org 25907, and 1-PP was demonstrated for the QC samples as
follows. Long-term stability for 13 months at -20º C has been demonstrated. Freezethaw stability in plasma was demonstrated after three freeze-thaw cycles. In-process
stability was demonstrated in human plasma for 24 hours at room temperature for
gepirone and for 6 hours for Org 25907. For 1-PP in-process stability is extrapolated
from the freeze thaw experiments in which the samples were at room temperature for
18.5 hours between freezing cycles. The Sponsor infers autosampler stability of
extracted samples by inclusion of QC samples that must meet acceptance criteria at the
beginning and end of each run.
According to the Method, the stability of the stock solutions is 6 months at 4º C. The
data are not shown.
In conclusion, the Organon bioanalytical method used for analysis of plasma samples (in
Study 28724) for gepirone, Org 25907, and 1-PP is adequately documented and
validated.

CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY AND BIOPHARMACEUTICS (OCPB) REVIEW

NDA: 21-164

Submission Dates: 5/18/01,
8/31/01
1/23/02
1/31/02
OCPB Receipt Dates: 5/25/01
9/17/01
1/24/02
2/1/02

Brand Name:

Ariza®

Generic Name:

Gepirone HCL; ORG 33062

Dosage Strength(s):

20, 40, 60, and 80 mg extended-release oral tablets

Indication:

Depression

Sponsor:

Organon Inc., West Orange, NJ

Type:

1S NDA (NME)

Primary Reviewer:

Gerald J. Fetterly, Ph.D.

Team Leader:

Ramana Uppoor, Ph.D.

Acting Team Leader: Vanitha Sekar, Ph.D.

I.
Executive Summary.
Recommendations.
The pharmacokinetic studies provided an understanding of the systemic exposure of
gepirone and its major metabolites and are considered acceptable for this NDA from the
Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics view provided the sponsor adequately
addresses the general and labeling comments. The General Comments given below and
the Labeling Comments should be conveyed to the sponsor.
General Comments.
1. In the pivotal bioequivalence study comparing different strengths of gepirone ER, the
40 and 80 mg strengths were not shown to be bioequivalent to the 20 mg tablets,
which were used in the clinical trials. No data were provided comparing the 60 mg
strength to the 20 mg strength of gepirone ER. Upon approval of gepirone ER, only
the 20 mg strength can be marketed.
2. In the food effect study, the data showed that food has a significant effect on gepirone
bioavailability (approximately increase in Cmax of 62%). This pronounced effect of
food on the bioavailability of gepirone suggests that food intake relative to
administration is crucial in affecting an augmentation in the bioavailability of the
drug. We recommend that the sponsor conduct an in vivo study evaluating the effects
of different meal compositions on gepirone ER pharmacokinetics. This information
would help provide appropriate labeling recommendations regarding gepirone
administration with respect to food consumption.
3. Throughout all of the pharmacokinetic studies, multiple peaks of gepirone and its
metabolites, along with high variability in the data, were observed following
administration. These fluctuations in absorption may be attributed to a pronounced
effect of food on the bioavailability of the drug and/or enterohepatic recycling of the
parent drug and its metabolites. We recommend in vitro assessment of Phase 2
metabolism since this may explain the presence of multiple peaks observed in patients
receiving gepirone.
4. A strong pharmacokinetic interaction was observed between gepirone and
ketoconazole (5-fold increase in gepirone concentrations). The effect of other
CYP3A4 inhibitors that can be potentially coadministered with gepirone ER has not
been evaluated. We recommend that the sponsor conduct an in vivo drug interaction
study to evaluate the effects of gepirone coadministration with an intermediate
inhibitor of CYP3A4, such as verapamil.
5. We recommend that the sponsor perform an in vitro assessment of (a) potential drugdrug interactions between gepirone and potent CYP3A4 inducers and (b) the drug’s
ability to induce CYP3A4 enzymes.
6. We recommend that the sponsor conduct an in vivo drug-drug interaction study with a
potent CYP2D6 inhibitor to assess its effects on the pharmacokinetics of gepirone and
its metabolites.
7. The results of the hepatic impairment study showed that the gepirone exposure was
augmented 2-fold, in conjunction with a 3- and 2-fold reduction in the Cmax and AUC
2

of 1-PP. The maintenance dose for gepirone in moderate hepatic impairment should
not exceed 40 mg/day. The pharmacokinetics of gepirone has not been adequately
characterized in severe hepatic impairment, thus it is recommended that the drug be
not administered to this patient population.
8. The results of the renal impairment study suggested that the plasma levels of gepirone
and its major metabolites were elevated by approximately 70% in patients with
moderate and severe renal impairment. The maintenance dose for gepirone in
moderate and severe renal impairment should not exceed 40 mg/day.
9. Gepirone plasma concentrations are increased 50-80% in the elderly. We recommend
that the maintenance dose in the elderly should not exceed 40 mg/day.
10. The dissolution specifications at 12 h and 20 h should be changed to 65-85% and
>85%, respectively.
11. We request that the sponsor clarify as to whether plasma gepirone (and any
metabolites) concentrations were measured in any of the pivotal efficacy trials
following administration of the IR and/or ER formulations of gepirone.
12. In all of the additional studies to be conducted, the sponsor should include gepirone
and its two major metabolites, 1-PP and 3’OH-gepirone in the analysis.
Labeling Comments.
See the proposed label for OCPB comments (p. 16)

Gerald J. Fetterly, Ph.D.

III.

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics Findings.

Organon Inc. is seeking approval of Ariza (Gepirone HCL) for the treatment of
depression in adults. Gepirone is an azapirone derivative that has both antidepressant and
anxiolytic properties. The parent drug is a relatively selective partial agonist at
serotonergic 5-HT1A receptors with lower affinity for 5-HT1B and other CNS receptors.
In addition, 3’OH-gepirone, a metabolite of gepirone, has illustrated antidepressant
activity as well. Bristol-Myers Squibb developed Gepirone originally as an immediate
release formulation. Following review of the safety data and to encourage compliance in
patients, an extended release formulation was developed by Bristol-Myers Squibb and
subsequently licensed to Organon. The results of the clinical trials suggest that a
minimum dose of 40 mg is efficacious. Thus, the recommended starting dose for Ariza
extended release (ER) tablets consists of 20 mg/day, administered as a single dose,
preferably in the morning. After assessing response to Ariza, the dose may be increased
up to a maximum dose of 80 mg/day in 20 mg increments.
The pharmacokinetic characteristics of gepirone are as follows:
• Poor absorption; F=14-17%
• Linear PK for ER tablets; 20-80 mg (high intra- and inter-subject variability)
• Steady-state PK; approaches nonlinearity at higher doses
• Plasma Protein binding; 72% for gepirone and 59% for 1-PP
• Terminal half-life; 2.5-3 h (4.5-12 h following ER administration)
• Metabolism; 1-PP and 3’OH-gepirone (active), which are both present at higher
plasma levels than parent drug.
• Mediated via CYP3A4 and to a lesser extent 2D6
• Other hydroxylated metabolites present in urine; mainly 3’5-dihyroxy-gepirone
• Elimination; 81% of administered dose in urine and 13% in feces

6

IV.

Question-Based Review.

1. Introduction
What Are the Highlights of the Chemistry and Physicochemical Properties of the
Drug?
Structure
The chemical name for gepirone is 3, 3 dimethyl-N-[4-[4-(2-pyrimidinyl)-1piperazinyl]butyl]glutarimide monohydrochloride.
The molecular formula is
C19H29N5O2+HCL and the molecular weight is 396 grams/mole. The chemical structure
is shown in Figure A:
Figure A. Chemical Structure of Gepirone HCL.

Formulation and Manufacturing
Gepirone is a white to off-white, crystalline powder, which is lipophilic in
character and highly soluble under acidic conditions. Gepirone ER tablets contain the
(b) (4)
active moiety, with colloidal silicon dioxide, red
oxide, hydroxypropyl
methylcellulose, microcrystalline cellulose, and magnesium stearate (Table C).
Table C. Composition of Gepirone ER Tablets (To-Be-Marketed).

(b) (4)

(b) (4)
(b) (4)

(b) (4)
(b) (4)
(b) (4)

Initially, Bristol-Myers Squibb developed Gepirone originally as an immediate
release formulation. Following review of the safety data and to encourage compliance in
patients, an extended release formulation was developed by Bristol-Myers Squibb (BMS)

8

and subsequently licensed to Organon. The final ER formulations developed by BMS
(b) (4)
(b) (4)
and
were used in the pivotal clinical trials.
is
(b) (4)
manufacturing the to-be-marketed formulation for Organon. The
ER formulation
(b) (4)
of gepirone, which is to-be-marketed, is
composition to the BMS product.
What Is the Proposed Mechanism of Action and Therapeutic Indication?
Gepirone is an azapirone derivative that has both antidepressant and anxiolytic
properties. The parent drug is a relatively selective partial agonist at serotonergic 5-HT1A
receptors with lower affinity for 5-HT1B and other CNS receptors. Gepirone is
metabolized primarily to 1-PP and 3’OH-gepirone. 3’OH-gepirone has illustrated
antidepressant activity as well. The proposed indication for gepirone is the treatment of
depression in adults.
What Is the Proposed Dosage Regimen?
The recommended starting dose for Ariza extended release tablets consists of 20
mg/day, administered as a single dose, preferably in the morning. After assessing
response to Ariza, the dose may be increased up to a maximum dose of 80 mg/day in 20
mg increments. The minimum effective dose observed is 40 mg/day.
2. Clinical Pharmacology
Was the bioanalytical method adequate for quantification of gepirone and any other
moieties?
In all of the pharmacokinetic studies that BMS conducted, gepirone was
administered orally and a validated GC/MS assay or a radioimmunoassay was used to
quantitate the drug and its metabolites. In contrast, Organon developed a LC/MS assay
that was utilized for the determination of gepirone and significant metabolite
concentrations. Based on the assay performance in all pharmacokinetic studies, the assay
is considered acceptable.
What Were the Clinical Endpoints Measured and Was An Exposure-Response
Relationship Established for Efficacy and Safety?
In order to evaluate the antidepressant activity of gepirone, the clinical endpoints
measured included the CGI (Clinical Global Impression) improvement score and the
Hamilton Depression scale. The clinical trial designs incorporated a flexible dose
schedule where the patients were dose-titrated to effect and subsequently doses were
decreased if any adverse events were observed. The sponsor analyzed the entire dose
range (20-80 mg) by comparing it to placebo, and 40-80 mg was considered to be
effective compared to placebo. The same analysis was performed for the observed
adverse events. In addition, the majority of the patients in the clinical trials received less
than 40 mg. Thus, a true dose-response relationship could not be determined for
gepirone.

9

Absorption and Distribution
Several studies have shown that gepirone has a high first pass effect following
oral absorption, resulting in poor bioavailability (F) of 14-17%. The absolute
bioavailability ranged from 6-34%, which is indicative of the high within and betweensubject variability. The parent drug is rapidly cleared from the plasma and has a large
volume of distribution, which can be attributed to its lipophilic structure. Across all of
the studies, various subjects exhibited multiple peaks of gepirone and its metabolites.
The plasma protein binding of gepirone and 1-PP were 72% and 59%, respectively.
Metabolism
Figure 1. Metabolic Scheme of Gepirone in Humans.

Following oral administration of gepirone, less than 9% of the radioactivity in
plasma was unchanged gepirone. In contrast, intravenous administration of the drug
resulted in greater than 50% of unchanged drug in the plasma. Thus, gepirone is
subjected to first pass metabolism by the liver and/or the gut wall. The biotransformation
pathway for gepirone consists of conversion to two major metabolites, 1-PP and 3’OHgepirone (Fig. 1). These two metabolites are present at higher concentrations in the
plasma compared to gepirone, and 3’OH is considered to be also active in addition to the
parent compound. 3’, 5 dihyroxy-gepirone is the predominant metabolite in the urine. In
addition, incubation of gepirone with ketoconazole, a potent CYP3A4 inhibitor, resulted
in complete suppression of 1-PP formation and a reduction in the formation of 3’OH10

gepirone to 17%. Based on in vitro assessment of the metabolism, these routes are
mediated by CYP3A4 and CYP2D6, with 3A4 being the predominant isozyme. Also,
several other metabolites have been detected in the plasma and urine.
Elimination
Following oral administration, gepirone has a t1/2 of approximately 3 h and is
primarily cleared from the bloodstream as metabolites in urine. Following administration
of the ER product, the terminal t1/2 is approximately 6-12 h.
Are the pharmacokinetics of gepirone linear with respect to dose?

Following single and multiple dose administration of the extended release
formulation, a trend was observed that Cmax and AUC showed a greater than proportional
increase with respect to dose. But, due to high variability in the data, both Cmax and AUC
were found to be proportional in the dose range of 20-80 mg.

11

What is the effect of Food on the pharmacokinetics of Gepirone?
The effect of food on the bioavailability of gepirone ER was investigated
following a single dose of 20 mg. The results showed that the Cmax and AUC were
increased by 62% and 24%, respectively in the presence of a high fat meal. Continuous
absorption was observed, suggesting no indication of dose dumping when gepirone ER is
administered with food.
However, in order to make appropriate labeling
recommendations regarding gepirone administration with respect to food intake, we
recommend that the sponsor conduct a study that evaluates different meal compositions
(See General Comments).
What is the inhibitory potential of Gepirone on various CYP450 isozymes?
Based on in vitro studies the inhibitory potential of gepirone and 1-PP on CYP450
isozymes was tested across a concentration range of 0-250 µM. As a result, the IC50 was
determined to be greater than 250 µM (90 µg/ml) for both compounds. Clinically, plasma
concentrations that were achieved following a dose of 80 mg (highest recommended
dose) did not exceed 40 ng/ml. Thus, the possibility that gepirone and 1-PP can inhibit
the activity of CYP450s is not plausible.
Do drug interactions exist between gepirone and ketoconazole, a potent inhibitor
CYP3A4?
A potential pharmacokinetic interaction was investigated between gepirone and
ketoconazole, a potent inhibitor of CYP3A4. Following a 20 mg dose of ER gepirone in
combination with ketoconazole (200 mg b.i.d.), the results illustrated that plasma
concentrations of the parent compound increased 5-fold while 1-PP decreased by
approximately 2-3-fold, in line with the metabolic conversion of gepirone to 1-PP via the
CYP3A4 route. In contrast, 3’OH metabolite concentrations increased 1.5-2-fold. As a
result, concomitant administration of gepirone with ketoconazole is not recommended.
Caution needs to be exercised when gepirone is coadministered with other drugs that are
also inhibited by CYP3A4, such as erythromycin, itraconazole, ritonavir, and nefazadone.
Dose reduction of gepirone may be warranted when gepirone is co-administered with
these drugs (See Labeling Comments).
Is there an age or gender effect on the pharmacokinetics of Gepirone?
Following multiple dose administration of 20, 40, and 80 mg of gepirone ER, no
significant gender differences were observed. In contrast, an age difference in
pharmacokinetic parameters was observed, with the elderly exhibiting higher values of
Cmax by 30% and 60% in men and women, and AUC by 50-80% in men and women,
compared with the young subjects. Thus, the maintenance dose of gepirone should not
exceed 40 mg/day in the elderly.
What is the effect of hepatic impairment on the pharmacokinetics of Gepirone?
In the hepatic impairment study following administration of a 20 mg dose of the
ER formulation, both gepirone and 1-PP pharmacokinetics were altered. The Cmax and
12

AUC of gepirone were augmented approximately 2-fold, in conjunction with a 3- and 2fold reduction in the Cmax and AUC of 1-PP in hepatically impaired subjects. The
gepirone maintenance dose should not exceed 40 mg/day in moderately impaired patients.
Although, gepirone plasma concentrations in patients with mild hepatic impairment were
increased, these differences were not statistically significant compared to normal subjects.
Patients with severe hepatic impairment have not been adequately studied.
Administration of gepirone to patients with severe hepatic impairment is not
recommended.
What is the effect of renal impairment on the pharmacokinetics of Gepirone?
In the renal impairment study following a single dose of 40 mg of the ER
formulation, plasma levels of gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH-gepirone increased in relation to
the level of renal impairment. The Cmax of gepirone increased by 74% in severely
impaired patients, while the AUC was increased by 65-71% in moderately and severely
impaired patients. In mildly impaired patients, the Cmax and AUC of gepirone and its
metabolites were elevated, but not statistically different from normal subjects. The
gepirone maintenance dose should not exceed 40 mg/day in both moderate and severely
impaired patients since the kidneys play a key role in the elimination of the metabolites of
gepirone.
What is the effect of poor and extensive metabolizers of dextromethorphan on the
pharmacokinetics of Gepirone?
Since gepirone is metabolized by CYP2D6 to a minor extent, a study was
conducted to evaluate the bioavailability in poor and extensive metabolizers of
dextromethorphan, using 15 mg b.i.d. of gepirone IR. No change was observed in Cmax
and AUC for gepirone in subjects with reduced activity of CYP2D6, but a 2.5 fold
increase was shown in the AUC for 1-PP. Poor metabolizers of dextromethorphan
compensated for the reduced metabolism of gepirone through the 2D6 pathway with an
increase in the conversion of gepirone to 1-PP probably via the 3A4 route. The sponsor
has not evaluated the effects of CYP2D6 inhibitors on the pharmacokinetics of gepirone.
We recommend that the sponsor conduct such a study in vivo (See General Comments).
What is the effect of race on the pharmacokinetics of Gepirone?
The effect of race was investigated following single and multiple dose
administration of 20 mg of gepirone ER. Gepirone plasma concentrations were 1.5-2fold higher in Caucasians, compared to Blacks and Asians. The results showed that the
difference in the extent of gepirone bioavailability was due to higher conversion of
gepirone to 1-PP in Black and Asian subjects. No dose adjustment is warranted in Blacks
and Asians since patients are dose-titrated to effect.

Are the different strengths of Gepirone ER (20, 40, and 80 mg) bioequivalent?
A bioequivalence study was performed by the sponsor to compare the
bioavailability of 1 x 40 mg with 2 x 20 mg and 1 x 80 mg with 4 x 20 mg tablets. The
results of that study indicated that the bioequivalence criterion was not met on Cmax and
AUC for the comparison between 1 x 40 mg vs. 2 x 20 mg (90% C.I. for Cmax; 1.02-1.35,
AUC; 0.99-1.33). In addition, the Cmax of the 1 x 80 mg was significantly higher than the
4 x 20 mg tablets (90% C.I. for Cmax; 0.96-1.26). The 40 mg and 80 mg strengths of
gepirone ER are not bioequivalent to the 20 mg ER strength. Thus, only the 20 mg
strength of gepirone ER can be marketed upon approval at this time.
Can the sponsor be granted an approval for the 60 mg strength of gepirone ER?
The sponsor has not submitted any data to address this question. Thus, the 60 mg
strength of gepirone ER is not recommended for approval.
Was the sponsor’s development of an IVIVC adequate for gepirone ER?
The sponsor attempted to develop an in vitro-in vivo correlation with the ER
(b) (4)
formulation.
The analysis performed was
inadequate to establish an IVIVC for this product.

2. Protocol 28718: Bioequivalence of Gepirone 20, 40, and 80 mg ER Tablets
(b) (4)
(
) in Healthy, Young, Extensive Metabolizers of Dextromethorphan,
Male Subjects.
Investigator.
S. De Bruyn, M.D., Wavre, Belgium.
Objectives.
The objective of this study was to (1) determine the bioequivalence of one 40 mg
gepirone ER tablet vs. 2 x 20 mg tablets and one 80 mg tablet vs. 4 x 20 mg tablets,
investigate the dose proportionality over the dose range 40-80 mg, and (3) investigate the
safety of the above mentioned dose regimens.
Study Design and Methods:
This study consisted of an open-label, randomized, four-way single dose,
crossover design. Each period was separated by a one-week washout period between
treatments. During each period, a single dose of one formulation strength of gepirone
was administered in the morning following a 10-h overnight fast and no food was
permitted until 4 h after administration. Serial blood samples were collected over a 72-h
period following each dose and were assayed for gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH-gepirone
concentration utilizing a validated LC/MS method.
Subjects:
Thirty-seven healthy male volunteers (age range: 18 - 50 yrs; all Caucasian) were
enrolled and thirty-two completed the study. The completed subjects received the
following treatments in a randomized order as a single dose:
(1) 1 x 40 mg ER tablet;
(2) 2 x 20 mg ER tablets;
(3) 1 x 80 mg ER tablet;
(4) 4 x 20 mg ER tablets.

54

Test Product, Dose, and Mode of Administration:
The test and reference formulations of gepirone were supplied to the investigator
(b) (4)
by
, which included:
(b) (4)
(b) (4)

(Lot # 0581394), containing 20 mg of
gepirone HCl. This strength was used as the reference product.
(b) (4)
(b) (4)
•
ER tablet formulation, product #
(Lot # 0300423), containing 40 mg of
gepirone HCl. This strength was a test formulation.
(b) (4)
(b) (4)
•
ER tablet formulation, product #
(Lot # 0300468), containing 80 mg of
gepirone HCl. This strength was also a test formulation.
Assay Validation:
An LC/MS assay was used to quantitate gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH in this study.
The calibration curves in plasma were linear for the concentration range from 0.1 – 10.0
ng/ml for gepirone, 0.20-10.0 ng/ml for 1-PP, and 0.25-10.0 ng/ml for 3’OH (r ≥ 0.993
for all analytes). Low, medium, and high QC samples were prepared at concentrations of
0.3, 4.0, and 8.0 ng/ml for gepirone and 3’OH, and 0.6, 4.0, and 8.0 ng/ml for 1-PP. The
deviations from the mean for gepirone were within 15.2% for the low QCs, 12.6% for the
medium QCs, and 13.1% for the high QCs. The corresponding values for 1-PP were
14.8%, 11.5%, and 14.0%, respectively. The corresponding values for 3’OH were 14.5%,
12.0%, and 13.2%, respectively.
Criteria for Evaluation:
Various pharmacokinetic parameters were determined for gepirone, 1-PP, and
3’OH-gepirone, which included Cmax, tmax, AUC, Vz, CL, and t1/2. In addition, they were
normalized to a 1-mg dose. Non-compartmental methods were used in the determination
of various pharmacokinetic parameters.
Statistical analysis was performed using an ANOVA for Cmax and AUC from
which the point estimates with their 90% confidence intervals were derived for average
bioequivalence testing. For tmax, the ANOVA was performed on ranks and the 95% nonparametric confidence interval of the true difference was calculated. Additionally,
bioequivalence testing was performed post-hoc using the population approach. Also,
dose proportionality was assessed using the same ANOVA using the data collected from
the 40 and 80 mg doses.

Results.

Table 5. Results of Average Bioequivalence Testing for 3’OH-gepirone.

Table 6. Inter- and Intra-subject Coefficients of Variation.

Average BE Testing.
When the 1 x 40 mg gepirone ER tablet was compared with 2 x 20 mg tablets, the
rate and extent of absorption was found to be equivalent for 1-PP and 3’OH-gepirone
(Tables 4, 5). In contrast, the Cmax and AUC for gepirone were significantly greater for
the 1 x 40 mg tablet compared to the 2 x 20 mg tablets; thus bioequivalence could not be
established between the 20 and 40 mg strengths (Table 3).
The sponsor also compared the 1 x 80 mg strength tablet to 4 x 20 mg tablets. For
all three compounds (gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH), bioequivalence was proven for AUC
between the test and reference (Tables 3-5). Cmax was also equivalent for the two
metabolites, but was not found to be equivalent for gepirone since a slightly higher Cmax
was observed for the 80 mg strength. Hence, BE could not be established between the 20
and 80 mg strengths either.

59

Table 7.

BE Testing using the Population Approach (Sponsor’s Analysis).
When comparing the 1 x 40 mg tablet with the 2 x 20 mg tablets using a
population BE approach, they were found to be bioequivalent with respect to Cmax and
AUC for all three compounds (Table 7).
Similarly, the 80 mg tablet was bioequivalent to the 4 x 20 mg tablets with respect
to both Cmax and AUC for all analytes.

As a result of an inspection of Study 28718 by the Division of Scientific
Investigation, the sponsor re-analyzed the data without some subjects whom experienced

61

evaluation did not change the original outcome. Thus, the average bioequivalence
criterion was not met for both Cmax and AUC when 1 x 40 mg and 2 x 20 mg tablets were
compared, as well as the 1 x 80 mg vs. the 4 x 20 mg tablets.
The incidence of adverse events was higher following intake of 1 x 80 mg
gepirone (93 AEs observed in 82% of the subjects) and after 4 x 20 mg (93 AEs observed
in 89% of the subjects) than after 1 x 40 mg (36 AEs observed in 63% of the subjects)
and after 2 x 20 mg (34 AEs observed in 52% of the subjects). These differences were
particularly observed for CNS, gastro-intestinal, and general disorders.
Conclusions.
The bioequivalence criteria were not met for the test tablets (20 vs. 40 mg and 20
vs. 80 mg). Dose proportionality could not be adequately assessed because only two
doses were considered in the statistical analysis. The 40 mg and 80 mg ER strengths of
gepirone are not approvable based on the results of this study.

4. Protocol FK-GBE-002: Effect of Food on the Bioavailability of Gepirone 20 mg
(b) (4)
ER Tablets (
).
Investigator.
(b) (4)

Objectives.
The objective of this study was to compare the bioavailability of gepirone under
fed and fasted conditions following a 20 mg dose.
Study Design and Methods:
This study was an open label, randomized, single-dose, two-way crossover design
that was balanced for first order residual effects (Fig. 7). Each 48 h treatment period was
separated by a five-day interval. During one session, doses were administered in the
morning following a 10 h overnight fast and no food was permitted until 4 h after dose

66

administration. In the alternate session, the subjects fasted overnight and received a
standard high fat breakfast in the morning; the dose of gepirone was administered within
15 min after completing the meal. Serial blood samples were collected over a 48 h period
following each dose (Table 11) and were assayed for gepirone, 1-PP, and 3-OH gepirone
utilizing a validated LC/MS method.
During the session when gepirone was administered with food, all subjects
received the same, standardized high fat breakfast, which consisted of:
• One buttered english muffin
• One fried egg
• One slice American cheese
• One slice Canadian bacon
• One serving of hash–brown potatoes
• Six fluid ounces of orange juice
• Eight fluid ounces of whole milk

Subjects:

(b) (4)
included:
• Gepirone HCl was prepared as modified release tablet formulation containing 20 mg
(b) (4)
of gepirone, product #
(Lot # 98-013T).
Assay Validation:
An LC/MS assay was used to quantitate gepirone and 1-PP in this study. For each
run, the calibration curves were linear for the concentration range from 0.1 – 25 ng/ml for
both compounds (r  0.998 for gepirone, r  0.996 for 1-PP, and r  0.992 for 3-OH).
Low medium, and high QC samples were prepared at concentrations of 0.15, 1.5, and 3.0
ng/ml for gepirone, at 1.5, 7.5, and 15 ng/ml for 1-PP, and at 0.75, 4, and 8 ng/ml for 3OH. The between day variability for the QC samples in all runs did not exceed 11.2% for
gepirone, 9.0% for 1-PP, and 15% for 3-OH. For the accuracy of the method, the
deviations from the mean for gepirone did not exceed 0.8% for the low QC sample, 1.4%
for the medium QC sample and 6.9% for the high QC sample. For 1-PP, the deviations
from the mean were 0.2% for the low QC sample, 0.5% for the medium QC sample, and
4.2% for the high QC sample. For 3-OH, the deviations from the mean were 3.3% for the
low QC sample, 0.5% for the medium QC sample, and 9.1% for the high QC sample.
Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of peak concentration
(Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax), area under the curve (AUC0-), half-life (t1/2) and
mean residence time (MRT).
Mean pharmacokinetic parameters were tested for differences due to sequence,
period, subject within sequence, and treatment utilizing an analysis of variance.
Statistical analysis was performed using two, one-sided t-test procedure on lntransformed Cmax and AUC and significance was set at the α=0.05 level.

Figure 10. Mean 3-OH Gepirone Plasma Concentrations.

Table 14. Mean (s.d.) PK Parameters for 3-OH Gepirone.

Following drug administration in the fasted state, two gepirone peaks were
observed at 3 and 5 h, with corresponding peaks of 3-OH gepirone at 5 and 7 h (Figs. 8,
10). The second peak may have resulted from the effect of food on the bioavailability of
the drug since food consumption was allowed after 4 h. Conversely, a second peak was
not observed in the subjects that took the drug with food. At 24 h, another peak was
observed in both the gepirone and 1-PP plasma profiles. The tmax for gepirone decreased
with food intake (Fig. 8). The plasma levels of gepirone following food consumption
exceeded those in the fasted state up to 16 h following drug administration. Following
food intake with drug administration, the bioavailability and peak plasma concentrations
of gepirone increased by 24% and 62%, respectively (Table 12). In addition, food
consumption increased the Cmax and AUC of 3-OH gepirone by 18% (Table 14).
Alternatively, the rate (Cmax) and extent (AUC) of absorption of 1-PP was unaffected by
food intake (Table 13).

71

Conclusions.
Following food consumption, the rate and extent of absorption of gepirone was
increased by 62 and 24%, respectively. In addition, the Cmax and AUC of 3-OH gepirone
increased by 18%, while they were unchanged for 1-PP. It is difficult to make a dosing
recommendation due to continuous absorption of the drug from the ER product, .
Note: The breakfast consumed by the subjects in this study deviates slightly from the
FDA recommended breakfast, but it seems reasonable. (The sponsor did not provide
caloric and fat content of meal).
5. Protocol CN105-046: Effect of Food (Timing of Meal) on the Bioavailability of
Extended Release Gepirone in Healthy male Volunteers.
Investigator.
(b) (4)

Objectives.
The objective of this study was to evaluate the effect of timing of food intake on
the pharmacokinetics of gepirone and 1-PP following administration of a 20 mg dose of
extended release (ER) gepirone using a 10 mg strength.
Study Design and Methods:
This study was an open label, randomized, single-dose, four-way crossover design
that was balanced for first order residual effects (Table 15). Each treatment period was
separated by a seven-day washout period. The drug was administered in the following
treatments, (1) 20 mg after an overnight fast, and a 10 h additional fast after dosing, (2)
20 mg gepirone 1 h prior to a standard meal, (3) 20 mg gepirone 15 min after a standard
meal, and (4) 20 mg gepirone 2 h after a standard meal. Except for the treatment meal,
the subjects did not receive food for 10 h after the dose. Serial blood samples were
collected over a 30 h period following each dose and were assayed for gepirone and 1-PP
utilizing a validated GC/MS method.
During the session when gepirone was administered with food, all subjects
received the same, standardized high fat breakfast, which was similar to the FDA
standard breakfast.
Subjects:
Twenty-eight healthy male volunteers (age range: 18 –40 yrs.; 27 Caucasian, and
1 Hispanic) participated in the study and were randomized into one of four treatment
groups consisting of 7 subjects/group.

Following administration of gepirone, in conjunction with food intake, Cmax and
AUC of gepirone increased by 69% and 32%, respectively following treatment 3
compared with treatment 1. Cmax was achieved between 3-6 h, and was significantly
greater for treatments 2, 3, and 4 compared to treatment 1 (Fig. 11, Table 16). After
treatments 2, 3, and 4, two peaks were observed, occurring at 3 and 6 h. This two peak

75

phenomenon was more pronounced for treatment 3, which suggested that the time of food
intake influenced this observation. One possible explanation was that food was directly
interacting with the release characteristics of the ER formulation. Since plasma
concentrations of gepirone in treatments 2, 3, and 4 did not fall below the levels observed
in treatment 1, the sponsor suggested that rapid release of the parent drug was not
probable.
For 1-PP, both Cmax and AUC were greater following treatments 3 and 4,
compared with treatments 1 and 2 (Fig. 12, Table 16). Tmax was observed between 5-6 h
following all regimens. In addition, only one peak plasma concentration was observed.

Since food consumption increases hepatic blood flow, resulting in a decrease in
metabolism of high extraction drugs, such as gepirone, the sponsor stated that the
metabolic ratio of Cmax and AUC should decrease in the presence of food. As a result, the
metabolic ratio of 1-PP to gepirone suggested that this value was decreased in the
presence of food (treatments 2 and 3), but was not statistically significant (Table 17).
Conclusions.
When gepirone ER was administered with food, the systemic exposure of
gepirone and 1-PP were higher than that observed under fasting conditions. The greatest
augmentation was observed following treatment 3 (drug administered within 15 min after
food consumption), where Cmax and AUC increased by 69% and 32%, respectively. In
addition, the incidences of adverse effects were lower in the presence of food (data not
shown). This is not a pivotal study since the 10 mg ER tablet is not proposed to be
marketed.

Following administration of the immediate release gepirone formulation, the drug
was rapidly absorbed without food consumption, indicated by the short tmax. In
conjunction with food, the AUC of gepirone increased by 48%. Cmax was achieved at
approximately 2 h compared to 0.75 h in the fasted state, but the values were not
significantly different from each other (Fig. 13, Table 19). In addition, two peaks were
observed following the treatment without food, occurring at 1 and 2.5 h. This two-peak
phenomenon was not observed in the fed state. With or without food intake, the t1/2 of
gepirone and 1-PP were approximately 3 and 6 h, respectively.
For 1-PP, both Cmax and AUC were not significantly different between the fed and
fasting states (Fig. 14, Table 19). Tmax was observed at approximately 1.25 h without
food intake, and at 3 h with food.
Conclusions.
These results suggested that a delay in the onset of absorption as well as a slower
rate of absorption of gepirone was associated with food intake. The presence of food also
resulted in a 48% increase in the extent of absorption (AUC) of gepirone.

7. Protocol CN105-026: Relative Bioavailability of Gepirone ER Tablets and IR
Capsules (BMS).
Objectives.
The objectives of this study were (1) to compare the pharmacokinetics of gepirone
and its metabolite, 1-PP, following administration of gepirone either as an immediate
release (IR) capsule or extended release (ER) tablet formulation, and (2) to determine
whether a 20 or 25 mg ER tablet dosed once a day could replace a 10 mg IR capsule
dosed twice daily (q12h).
Study Design and Methods:
This study consisted of an open, randomized, 4-session crossover design balanced
for first order residual effects. Each session was separated by a 7 day interval. Each
subject qualified for study entry based on medical history and the inclusion/exclusion
criteria. All subjects fasted the night prior to dosing and were administered gepirone as
specified in the protocol. The subjects were allowed to consume food at 4 h following
drug administration. Serial blood samples were collected pre-dose and up to 30 h
following gepirone dosing for the determination of pertinent pharmacokinetic parameters.
Following administration of the IR formulation, the 12 h blood sample was collected
prior to dosing. All samples were analyzed to determine the content of gepirone and 1PP, one of the major circulating metabolites.
Subjects:
Twelve healthy male volunteers (age range: 19 –36 yrs.; 10 Caucasian, 1 black
and 1 oriental) participated in the study and were divided into 4 groups consisting of 3
subjects/group.

81

Assay Validation:
The GC/MS assay was used to quantitate gepirone and 1-PP in this study. The
calibration curve was linear for the concentration range from 0.1 – 25 ng/ml for both
compounds (r > 0.996). Low and high QC samples were prepared at concentrations of
2.17 and 20.68 ng/ml for gepirone and at 1.92 and 18.28 ng/ml for 1-PP. The between
day variability for the QC samples was determined, which did not exceed 6.03% for
gepirone and 7.73% for 1-PP. For the accuracy of the method, the deviations from the
mean for gepirone were 10.8% for the low QC sample and 10.1% for the high QC
sample. For 1-PP, the deviations from the mean were 7.96% for the low QC sample and
5.46% for the high QC sample.

82

Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of peak concentration
(Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax), area under the curve (AUC0-30 h), elimination
half-life (t1/2), and the fraction of gepirone absorbed over time. Non-compartmental
methods were used in the determination of various pharmacokinetic parameters and
evaluation of the absorption kinetics for gepirone was performed using the method
described by Wagner and Nelson.
An ANOVA, incorporating sequence, period and treatment effects, was used for
the statistical analysis of various pharmacokinetic parameters for both gepirone and 1-PP.
If the test for treatment effects was significant, then all pairwise comparisons of the
means were performed using Tukey’s multiple comparisons procedure (α=0.05). If there
was significant heterogeneity of variance, then a weighted ANOVA was performed and
then treatment means were compared using unequal variance t-tests.

Following administration of the ER formulation, tmax for both gepirone and 1-PP
ranged from 4.8 – 5.6 h, compared with 1 – 2 h for the IR form (Figs 15, 16, Table 21).
For both gepirone and 1-PP, the Cmax was similar for all of the ER formulations,
indicating the lack of dose-dumping or premature release. The Cmax for the IR
formulation was 3-fold greater than the ER form. The gepirone AUC was similar among
all formulations. In contrast, the 1-PP AUC was statistically greater following
administration of the IR formulation, compared to all ER forms. In addition, two peaks
were observed following administration of the ER formulation of gepirone. Possible
explanations for this phenomenon by the sponsor (BMS) included that the drug was in the
distal part of the small intestine accounting for an increase in the bioavailability (Study
CN105-012), increased absorption due to food consumption at 4 h after drug
administration, or enterohepatic recycling of unchanged drug.

85

Figure 17. Mean Fraction of Gepirone Absorbed over Time (Wagner-Nelson
Method).

(b) (4)

The fraction of gepirone absorbed over time revealed that greater than 90% of the
drug is not absorbed until after 20 h following administration of the ER formulation (Fig.
17). Thus, the ER form allows for continuous absorption of gepirone.
In addition, the sponsor indicated that t1/2 values following ER dosing were
approximately 21, 30, and 13 h for gepirone and 11, 13, and 15 h for 1-PP, respectively.
The augmentation in t1/2 values illustrates the phenomenon of flip-flop pharmacokinetics,
which accounts for the drug being eliminated at a faster rate than it is absorbed. This
observation is a typical characteristic of ER formulations.
Conclusions.
The bioavailability of gepirone following once daily dosing of the ER form was
comparable to b.i.d. dosing of the IR form. But, the overall exposure (AUC) of 1-PP was
not similar comparing the ER to the IR form. Following ER administration, the best
rationale to explain the two peaks observed in this study may be due to food
consumption. If enterohepatic recycling could explain this observation, the two peaks
should be observed independent of whether gepirone was administered in the IR or ER
form.

86

8. Protocol CN105-060: Comparative Bioavailability of Gepirone ER Tablets and
Gepirone Solution.
Objectives.
The objectives of this study were (1) to provide a comparison of the oral
bioavailability of the 5 mg, 10 mg, and 20 mg gepirone extended release (ER) tablets
relative to a gepirone reference solution following daily dosing (20 mg) to steady-state
and (2) to determine whether four 5 mg ER tablets, two 10 mg ER tablets, and one 20 mg
ER tablet are bioequivalent under steady-state conditions.
Study Design and Methods:
This study consisted of a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, crossover
design. The study design included four 7-day treatment periods without an intervening
washout period. Each session was separated by a 7-day interval. Each subject qualified
for study entry based on medical history and the inclusion/exclusion criteria. All subjects
fasted the night prior to each dose and were administered gepirone daily for 28 days. The
subjects were allowed to consume food at 4 h following drug administration. Serial
blood samples were collected pre-dose and up to 24 h following gepirone administration
of the morning dose on days 7, 14, 21, and 28. The 24 h sample on days 8, 15 and 22 was
taken prior to the administration of the morning dose for that day. Single blood samples
were taken on days 5 and 6 (session 1), 12 and 13 (session 2), 19 and 20 (session 3), and
26 and 27 (session 4) before administration of the morning dose for determination of Cmin
data and attainment of steady-state. During the period when the solution was
administered, blood samples for determination of gepirone and 1-PP concentrations were
obtained immediately prior to dose and at 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 4, 6, 8, and 12 h after each dose
on day 7. For the determination of pertinent pharmacokinetic parameters, all samples
were analyzed to determine the concentration of gepirone and 1-PP, one of the major
circulating metabolites.
Subjects:
Forty-eight healthy male volunteers (age range: 19 –36 yrs; 10 subjects completed
the trial in groups A, C, and D, while 9 subjects completed the trial in group B) were
enrolled and 39 completed the study and were divided into 4 groups consisting of 12
subjects/group (10 on active drug, 2 on placebo).
Test Product, Dose, and Mode of Administration:
The formulations of gepirone ER tablets and an IR solution that were supplied to
the investigator by the Pharmaceutical Product Development Division of Bristol-Myers
Squibb Co. include:

Assay Validation:
A GC/MS assay was used to quantitate gepirone and 1-PP in this study. The
sponsor stated that each analytical run resulted in two output files containing the results
for either gepirone or 1-PP. For each run, the calibration curves were linear for the
concentration range from 0.1 – 25 ng/ml for both compounds (r > 0.991 for gepirone and
r > 0.998 for 1-PP). Low and high QC samples were prepared at concentrations of 2.03
88

and 18.3 ng/ml for gepirone and at 1.82 and 19.8 ng/ml for 1-PP. In order to complete
the study analysis, a second set of QC samples were prepared because it became apparent
that the sponsor had an insufficient quantity of the original QCs. In this second set, the
gepirone concentrations for the low and high QCs were 2.08 and 20.8 ng/ml, respectively.
The 1-PP concentrations for the low and high QCs were 2.09 and 20.9 ng/ml,
respectively. The between day variability for the QC samples in all runs did not exceed
9.0% for gepirone and 16.0% for 1-PP. For the accuracy of the method, the deviations
from the mean for gepirone did not exceed 8.2% for the low QC sample and 6.3% for the
high QC sample. For 1-PP, the deviations from the mean were 4.63% for the low QC
sample and 3.03% for the high QC sample.
Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of peak concentration
(Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax), area under the curve (AUC0-24 h for the ER tablets
and AUC0-12 h for the solution), trough plasma concentrations measured prior to drug
administration (Cmin), average plasma concentration (Cavg), and F.I. (fluctuation index,
calculated by (Cmax-Cmin/Cavg)).
An ANOVA, incorporating effects for sequence, subjects nested within sequence
group, treatment, period, the adjusted period by treatment interaction and the residual
error term was used for the statistical analysis of various pharmacokinetic parameters for
both gepirone and 1-PP. A repeated measures ANOVA followed by appropriate multiple
comparisons procedures was used to assess the attainment of steady-state. The two onesided tests procedure, through the use of 90% confidence intervals, was used to assess the
relative bioavailability of the ER formulations with respect to the IR solution, and of the 4
x 5 mg ER, 2 x 10 mg ER, and the 1 x 20 mg ER with respect to each other.

The average plasma concentrations of both parent drug and metabolite were
similar following administration of the three ER tablet formulations (Figs. 18, 19, Tables
23, 24). In contrast, the solution showed higher peak and lower trough concentrations.
For the ER formulations, the tmax occurred between 3 and 4 h, while the tmax for the
solution was less than 1 h. In addition, the plasma concentration-time profile for gepirone
illustrated two peaks, occurring at approximately 3 and 5 h (Fig. 18). The ER
formulations had significantly less peak-to-trough fluctuation than the reference solution
(Table 23). Following b.i.d. dosing of the solution, the 2nd peak was approximately 25%
less than the 1st peak (Fig. 18).
When the bioavailability results were compared, the rate (Cmax) and extent of
absorption (AUC) for both the parent drug and metabolite were similar for all ER
formulations (Table 23). In comparison to the solution, all three ER formulations
demonstrated lower overall bioavailability. Based on gepirone’s AUC, the ER tablets
were approximately 80% bioavailable relative to the solution.
For 1-PP, the
bioavailability estimates were only about 20% lower (Table 24).
Conclusions.
Following ER administration, two peaks were observed and the second peak
occurred at approximately 1 h following food consumption. The best rationale to explain
(b) (4)
the two peaks observed in this study may be due to food consumption.
. In addition, the rate and extent of absorption of
both gepirone and 1-PP were comparable for all three ER forms. But, the overall
exposure (AUC) of gepirone was not similar compared with the solution. Consistent with
administration of ER formulations, all tablet strengths exhibited significantly lower peak

11. Greenblatt Study: Human Cytochromes Mediating Gepirone Biotransformation
at Low Substrate Concentrations.
Background.
Prior to this study, this group performed in vitro experiments to assess which
CYP450 isozymes where involved in the metabolism of gepirone (von Moltke et al,
1998). The results of the study concluded that CYP450 3A4 and 2D6 were the main
enzymes involved in metabolizing the parent drug. But, the experiments were performed
with therapeutically irrelevant concentrations of gepirone. Thus, the following study was
conducted to ascertain a similar objective at clinically relevant concentrations of the drug.
Objectives.
The objective of this study was to determine which cytochrome enzymes mediate
the biotransformation of gepirone.
Study Design and Methods:
Microsomal preparations from a human liver were used for this study. The donor
was established to be a CYP2D6 normal metabolizer phenotype. The microsomes were
prepared by an ultracentrifugation method. Various concentrations of gepirone ranging
from 0 – 1000 nM were prepared. Additional 1000 nM samples were studies with
coaddition of ketoconazole (3A inhibitor) and quinidine (2D6 inhibitor). Also,
microsomes containing human CYP3A4 or 2D6 expressed by cDNA-transfected human
lymphoblastoid cells were used.
Assay Validation:

97

The LC/ESI-MS assay was developed to quantitate gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH
gepirone in this study. The calibration curves were linear for the concentration range
from 0.36 – 9 ng/ml, 10 – 500 ng/ml, and 50 – 300 ng/ml for gepirone; 0.36 – 9.0 ng/ml
and 0.25 –10 ng/ml for 3’OH gepirone; and 0.25 – 10 ng/ml for 1-PP (r > 0.995 for all
compounds). Each standard curve had a minimum of five calibration points, with a
relative standard error within ±15%.
Results.
Figure 21. Formation Rates of 1-PP and 3’OH gepirone in relation to the Gepirone
Concentration.

Both 1-PP and 3’ OH gepirone were formed in vitro at substrate concentrations of
50 nM or higher, with the rate of formation for 1-PP generally exceeding those of 3’OH
gepirone (Fig. 21). When gepirone was co-incubated with ketoconazole, the inhibitor
completely eliminated the formation of 1-PP, and reduced the formation of 3’OH
gepirone by approximately 80% (data not shown). Co-incubation of gepirone with
quinidine, a potent inhibitor of CYP2D6 showed no inhibition in the formation of either
1-PP or 3’OH gepirone (data not shown).

98

Figure 22. Chemical Structures of Gepirone and its Hydroxylated Metabolites.

From the results of the chromatograms and LC/MS/MS analysis, the metabolites
were identified as 3’OH gepirone, 5-OH gepirone, 2-OH gepirone, 3’, 5 diOH gepirone,
and Org 25945 (Fig. 22). The structural assignments were verified by comparison of their
LC/MS/MS spectra to that of a corresponding authentic standard.
Additional chromatographic peaks identified as 5-OH and 2-OH gepirone were also
observed as in vitro metabolites, but were not quantitated since reference standards
were not available.
Conclusions.
Based on an in vitro assessment, the results showed that CYP450 3A4 and 2D6
are involved in the metabolism of gepirone at therapeutically relevant concentrations. In
addition, CYP3A4 may be solely responsible for the formation of 1-PP and 3’OH
gepirone. CYP2D6 plays a minimal role in the formation of 3’OH and 5-OH gepirone.
Also, several mono- and di-OH metabolites of gepirone were verified in vitro by an
LC/MS/MS assay.
12. Von Moltke Study: Gepirone and 1-PP: Cytochrome Inhibitory Effects.
Objectives.
The objective of this study was to determine the potential inhibitory activity of
gepirone and 1-PP on various CYP450 isozymes.

99

Study Design and Methods:
Using pooled human microsomes, the potential inhibitory effect of gepirone and
1-PP on the activity of specific human cytochromes was evaluated using the following
index reactions and methods: 1A2, phenacetin to acetaminophen; 2C9, phenytoin to paraOHphenyl hydantoin; 2C19, S-mephenytoin to 4-OH mephenytoin; 2D6,
dextromethorphan to dextrorphan; 2E1, chlorzoxazone to 6-OH-chlorzoxazone; 3A,
dextromethorphan to 3-methoxymorphinan. Varying concentrations of gepirone and 1-PP
(0-250µM) were coincubated with fixed concentrations of the index substrate. Also, the
studies were conducted with positive control index inhibitors (α-naphthoflavone for 1A2;
sulfaphenazole for 2C9; omeprazole for 2C19; quinidine, fluoxetine, and norfluoxetine
for 2D6; diethyldithiocarbamate for 2E1; and ketoconazole and norfluoxetine for 3A).
Results.
Table 25. Inhibition of CYP450s by Gepirone, 1-PP, and positive control index
inhibitors.

For all of the CYP450 enzymes, the gepirone and 1-PP concentration needed to
inhibit 50% of the enzyme activity exceeded 250 µM (90 µg/ml) (Table 25).
Conclusions.
Both gepirone and 1-PP appear not to inhibit any of the CYP450 enzymes at the
concentrations tested. Clinically, plasma concentrations that were achieved following a
dose of 80 mg (highest to be marketed dose) exceeded 40 ng/ml. Thus, the possibility
that gepirone and 1-PP can inhibit the activity of CYP450s is not plausible.

(b) (4)

Objectives.
The objective of this study was to determine and compare the in vitro binding of
gepirone and 1-PP to plasma proteins in mouse, rat, dog, and human using an
ultrafiltration method.
Study Design and Methods:
The fraction of drug bound was determined using ultrafiltration. Plasma was
collected from each species. Solutions of 14C-gepirone and 14C-1-PP were prepared, and
then an appropriate amount of each compound was added to plasma producing final drug
concentrations ranging from 30 - 150 ng/ml for gepirone and 25 – 130 ng/ml for 1-PP.
The samples were mixed and equilibrated for 10 min. An aliquot from each sample was
centrifuged in an ultrafiltration device at 1500 x g for 30 min and analyzed for 14C using a
Packard 1900CA liquid scintillation analyzer (Downers Grove, Ill.). The percent of drug
bound was calculated as %bound = ((dpm in retentate –dpm in ultrafiltrate)/(dpm in
retentate)) x 100.

In humans, gepirone and 1-PP were approximately 72% and 59% bound to plasma

Conclusions.

plasma proteins.

Objectives.
The objective of this study was to determine the effect of gepirone on the plasma
protein binding of potentially co-administered drugs.
Study Design and Methods:
The fraction of drug bound was determined using ultrafiltration. Plasma was
collected from each species. Solutions of 14C-gepirone and 14C-1-PP were prepared, and
then an appropriate amount of each compound was added to plasma producing final drug
concentrations ranging from 30 - 150 ng/ml for gepirone and 25 – 130 ng/ml for 1-PP.
102

The samples were mixed and equilibrated for 10 min. An aliquot from each sample was
centrifuged in an ultrafiltration device at 1500 x g for 30 min and analyzed for 14C using a
Packard 1900CA liquid scintillation analyzer (Downers Grove, Ill.). The percent of drug
bound was calculated as %bound = ((dpm in retentate –dpm in ultrafiltrate)/(dpm in
retentate)) x 100.

15. Protocol CN105-049: Pharmacokinetics and Linearity During Single and
Multiple Dose Administration of Gepirone ER.
Objectives.
The objective of this study was to evaluate the safety, tolerability and
pharmacokinetics of gepirone after single and multiple dose administration as the
extended release (ER) formulation.
Study Design and Methods:
This study was a double-blind parallel group, placebo-controlled design in which
five groups of 12 subjects received 5 dose levels of ER gepirone (Table 28). After the
low dose was judged to be safe and tolerated, the next higher dose was administered.
Each treatment period was separated by a seven-day washout period. Serial blood
samples were collected over a 48 h period following a single dose of gepirone ER on day
1 and for 48 h after the last dose on day 8 during the multiple dose segment of the study
(days 3-8). On days 1 and 8, the subjects remained fasted for 4 h following drug
administration. The samples were assayed for gepirone and 1-PP utilizing a validated
GC/MS method.
Subjects:
Sixty healthy male volunteers (age range: 18 –40 yrs; 30 Caucasian, 27 black, and
3 Hispanic) were enrolled in the study. Of the sixty subjects, only two were poor
metabolizers and the rest of them were extensive metabolizers of dextromethorphan.
Each subject was randomized to five treatment groups, consisting of 9/treatment group
and 3 subjects that received placebo.
Test Product, Dose, and Mode of Administration:
The test formulation of gepirone ER capsules was supplied to the investigator by
Bristol-Myers Squibb Co., which included:
(b) (4)
• ER tablet formulation, product #
(Lot #E89F090), containing 10 mg

Assay Validation:
The GC/MS assay was used to quantitate gepirone and 1-PP in this study. The
calibration curve was linear for the concentration range from 0.1 – 25 ng/ml for both
compounds (r = 0.998 for gepirone and r = 0.999 for 1-PP). Low and high QC samples
were prepared at concentrations of 1.98 and 18.3 ng/ml for gepirone and 2.18 and 20.1
ng/ml for 1-PP. In addition, QC samples were prepared just prior to sample analysis at 2
and 20 ng/ml for both compounds. The between day variability for the QC samples did
not exceed 4.62% for gepirone and 4.51% for 1-PP. For the accuracy of the method, the
deviations from the mean for gepirone were 5.5% for the low QC sample and 6.93% for
the high QC sample. For 1-PP, the deviations from the mean were 4.86% for the low QC
sample and 4.57% for the high QC sample.
Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of peak concentration
(Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax), half-life (t 1/2), trough concentration (Cmin), area
under the curve (AUC0-24 h), and an accumulation ratio for Cmax and AUC (AR). Noncompartmental methods were used in the determination of various pharmacokinetic
parameters.
Dose linearity and proportionality of Cmax and AUC was evaluated statistically by
weighted linear regression. The test for dose linearity was based on the t-test for a linear
trend across doses. Statistical assessment of the attainment of steady-state by Cmin data
was conducted in a randomized block ANOVA model. Differences in the AR were
evaluated using a one-way ANOVA. All tests of significance were performed at the p =
0.05 level.

105

Results.
Figure 23. Mean Plasma Concentrations of Gepirone on Day 1.

Figure 24. Mean Steady-state Plasma Concentrations of Gepirone on Day 8.

(b) (4)

106

Table 29. Mean Pharmacokinetic Parameters of Gepirone Following a Single Dose
(Day 1) and on Dosing to Steady State (Day 8).

Following once daily dosing of gepirone ER, an initial peak plasma concentration
occurred at approximately 2-3 h, but an increase in absorption following food intake at 4
h resulted in a tmax at 5h on both days 1 and 8 (Figs. 23, 24, Table 29). The apparent t1/2
for gepirone ranged from 4 –11 h on day 1, and 6 –12 h at steady state (day 8). For
gepirone, linear regression analysis of Cmax and AUC vs. dose on day 1 revealed a lack of
fit for Cmax and a suggestion of linearity for AUC (r2 = 0.453). The relationship between
dose and AUC or Cmax improved at steady state (r2 = 0.64, 0.63). Modest accumulation
of gepirone levels was observed following once daily dosing of the ER formulation.

107

Table 30. Mean Pharmacokinetic Parameters of 1-PP Following a Single Dose (Day
1) and on Dosing to Steady State (Day 8).

For 1-PP, Cmax was achieved at 5 h independent of single dose or steady state
conditions (Fig. 24, Table 30). The apparent t1/2 ranged from 7.5 – 10.5 h. AUC and
Cmax were marginally proportional to escalating doses on both days 1 and 8, respectively
(r2 = 0.706 and 0.585 for Cmax, r2 = 0.582 and 0.448 for AUC). No differences were
observed in the metabolic ratio, thus suggesting that disposition of gepirone and 1-PP was
not influenced by the dose administered.
Conclusions.
Based on Cmin values, steady state conditions were achieved on day 2 (data not
shown). Modest accumulation of gepirone and 1-PP following once daily dosing was
observed. In addition, assessment of dose linearity with Cmax and AUC after single dose
and steady state conditions was inconclusive due to high pharmacokinetic variability and
multiple plasma peaks of gepirone. One possible explanation for the variability observed
in this study could be due to the effect of food consumption, resulting in an alteration of
the bioavailability of the parent compound.

108

16. Protocol CN105-068: Steady-State Pharmacokinetics of Gepirone ER Tablets in
Young and Elderly Healthy Male and Female Subjects.
Objectives.
The objectives of this study were to evaluate the effects of age and gender on the
steady-state pharmacokinetics of gepirone and 1-PP following administration of gepirone
ER.
Study Design and Methods:
This study consisted of a double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel treatment
design. All subjects fasted 10 h prior to dosing and were administered ER gepirone as
specified in the protocol. Each subject received 20 mg (1 x 20 mg tablet) or matching
placebo for days 1-7, 40 mg (2 x 20 mg tablets) or matching placebo for days 8-14, and
80 mg (4 x 20 mg tablets) or matching placebo for days 15-21. There was no washout
period between dose levels. On all study days except days 7, 14, and 21, the subjects
continued to fast for 2 h after dosing. On days 7, 14, and 21, the subjects continued to
fast for 4 h after dosing. Serial blood samples were collected pre-dose and up to 24 h
following gepirone administration of the morning dose on days 7, 14, and 21. Additional
blood samples were taken on days 4, 5, 6; 11, 12, 13; and 18, 19, and 20 to check the
attainment of steady-state. The pre-dose samples on days 7, 14, and 21 also were used as
Cmin samples. All samples were analyzed to determine the concentration of gepirone and
1-PP, one of the major circulating metabolites.
Subjects:
Sixty healthy volunteers, 15 men and 15 women in the age range of 18-40 years,
and 15 men and 15 women in the age range of 65-80 years participated in the trial. Of the
15 subjects in each demographic group, 12 received gepirone ER and 3 received matching
placebo tablets.
Test Product, Dose, and Mode of Administration:
The test formulation of gepirone ER tablets was supplied to the investigator by
Bristol-Myers Squibb Co., which included:
(b) (4)
• ER tablet formulation, product #
(Lot #E90H381), was a pink,
modified oval-shaped, biconvex tablet containing 20 mg of gepirone HCl for
(b) (4)
(b)
extended release (
= (4) h).
(b) (4)
• Placebo tablet, product #
, was used to match the gepirone ER tablet.
Assay Validation:
A GC/MS assay was used to quantitate gepirone and 1-PP in this study. The
calibration curve was linear for the concentration range from 0.1 – 25 ng/ml for both
compounds (r ≥ 0.990). Multiple QC samples were prepared at concentrations of 2, 8, 20,
and 40 ng/ml for both gepirone and 1-PP. For gepirone, the between day and within day
109

assay variability were less than 3.6% and 7.0%, respectively. The corresponding values
for 1-PP were 5.4% and 3.4%, respectively.
Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of peak concentration
(Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax), area under the curve (AUC0-t), and a metabolic
ratio for Cmax and AUC. Non-compartmental methods were used in the determination of
various pharmacokinetic parameters.
In order to assess the age and gender effects on the pharmacokinetics, an
ANOVA, incorporating the appropriate covariates was performed. Weighted linear
regression was used to test the dose proportionality of Cmax and AUC, and a subsequent tstatistic was conducted to determine significance. For the determination of the onset of
steady-state, a randomized block ANOVA was used for assessment of Cmin values. With
the exception of Levene’s test, significance was set at p=0.05 level.
Results.
Figure 25. Mean Plasma Gepirone Concentrations (Day 1 and 7).

Table 31. Mean (SD) Pharmacokinetic Parameters of Gepirone.

111

Table 32. Mean (SD) Pharmacokinetic Parameters for 1-PP.

Figure 27. Mean (SD) Cmin Values of Gepirone for the 4 Demographic Groups.

112

Figure 28. Mean (SD) Cmin Values of 1-PP for the 4 Demographic Groups.

The pharmacokinetics of gepirone were analyzed in four demographic groups
(elderly male and female and young male and female) at the dose levels of 20, 40, and 80
mg. For gepirone, the tmax ranged from 2.5-5 h and trough concentrations were stable at
each dose level (Fig. 25, Table 31). As a result, day 7, 14, and 21 were representative of
steady-state data for both gepirone and 1-PP (Figs 27, 28). Following the 80 mg dose, a
trend was observed that Cmax and AUC exhibited a greater than proportional increase with
respect to dose (Fig. 25, Table 31). Due to high variability in the population, no
statistically significant differences were observed in dose corrected Cmax and AUC. The
AUC values in elderly females at the three doses were 7, 12, and 45% greater compared
with those observed with young females. In contrast, no gender effect was observed with
steady state AUCs in the young population. Thus, differences in Cmax and AUC were age
related, and not gender related.
For 1-PP, the tmax ranged from 2.5 –5 h also (Fig. 26, Table 32). In contrast to
gepirone, both Cmax and AUC were proportional with respect to dose over the entire dose
range. As a result, the pharmacokinetics of 1-PP were not influenced by age and gender.
Although, a decrease in the 1-PP/gepirone molar ratio was observed at higher doses,
suggesting that metabolic saturation may have occurred.
The most common adverse events following gepirone administration were
lightheadedness and headache. In addition, systolic hypotension was prevalent in 11/13
young males, 6/12 elderly females, and 12/13 young females on active drug. Diastolic
hypotension was prominent in 9/13 young females on active drug.

113

Conclusions.
Statistical analysis of the gepirone data, in conjunction with high variability in the
data, showed that both Cmax and AUC were dose proportional over the 20-80 mg dose
range, with the exception of the relationship between Cmax and dose in elderly females.
Also, no gender differences were observed, but the data revealed an age difference in
pharmacokinetic parameters, with the elderly exhibiting higher Cmax and AUC values
compared with the young subjects. For 1-PP, dose proportionality was demonstrated in
all four treatment groups, and neither age nor gender differences were observed in 1-PP
pharmacokinetic parameters. Since the metabolic ratio decreased at higher doses, a
decrease in the metabolism of gepirone could occur at higher doses. It is recommended
that the maximum daily dose of gepirone should not exceed 40 mg in the elderly.
17. Protocol FK-GBE-005: Single Dose Pharmacokinetics of Gepirone 20 mg MR
(b) (4)
