) in Normal and Hepatically Impaired Volunteers.
Objectives.
The objective of this study was to compare the pharmacokinetics of gepirone and
its major metabolites in normal and hepatically impaired patients.
Study Design and Methods:
This study consisted of an open-label, single-dose, two-treatment design. In
session 1, 12 hepatically impaired patients received a single 20 mg dose of gepirone ER
following a 10 h fast. In session 2, 12 matched control subjects were administered a
single dose of 20 mg of gepirone ER. Serial blood samples were collected over 48 h and
urine was collected for 24 h. All samples were assayed for gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OHgepirone content using LC/MS. In addition, the sponsor made an effort to more fully
define the presence of additional metabolites.
Subjects:
Twenty-five subjects (age 18-70; 12 Caucasian and 13 Hispanic), with hepatic
impairment as well as healthy subjects, were enrolled in the study. Twenty-four subjects
completed the study, consisting of two groups of 12/group. The hepatically impaired
subjects included 7 subjects with Grade A, 5 subjects with Grade B, and 1 subject with
Grade C hepatic impairment based on the Child-Pugh scores recorded for each subject.
Test Product, Dose, and Mode of Administration:
(b) (4)
The test formulation of gepirone ER tablets was supplied to the
, which included:
(b) (4)
• MR tablet formulation, product #
(Lot #98-013T), containing 20 mg of gepirone
HCl.

114

Assay Validation:
An LC/MS/MS assay was used to quantitate gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH-gepirone
in this study. The calibration curves were linear for the concentration range from 0.0259.0 ng/ml for gepirone, 0.1 – 10 ng/ml for 1-PP, and 0.25-15 ng/ml for 3’OH-gepirone (r
≥ 0.996, r ≥ 0.996, and r ≥ 0.997 for gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH). Low, medium, and high
QC samples were prepared at concentrations of 0.5, 1.0, and 2.0 ng/ml for gepirone and
1-PP, and 0.25, 5.0, and 10 ng/ml for 3’OH-gepirone. The deviations from the mean for
gepirone were within 14.0% for the low QCs, 6.8% for the medium QCs, and 7.2% for
the high QCs. The corresponding values for 1-PP were 8.1%, 11.4%, and 12.1%,
respectively. The corresponding values for 3’OH-gepirone were 7.0%, 10.2%, and
10.1%, respectively.
Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of peak concentration
(Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax), area under the curve (AUC0-t), half-life (t1/2), and
the terminal elimination rate constant (Ke). Non-compartmental methods were used in
the determination of various pharmacokinetic parameters.
An ANOVA, incorporating group, gender, weight, and age was utilized in order to
compare Cmax, t1/2, AUC, and Ke. In addition, Cmax and AUC also were log-transformed
for comparison of the two groups. In all tests, differences were considered significant if p
 0.05 (2-tailed test). Also for practical considerations, 24 subjects (12/group) were
employed to show an effect difference of 50%.

For gepirone, both Cmax and AUC were significantly higher in the hepatically
impaired population compared to the healthy volunteers (Fig. 29, Table 33). In the
subjects with hepatic impairment, AUC was increased approximately 2-fold compared
with normal volunteers. The sponsor suggested that this increase is primarily attributable
to high AUC values for subjects 1, 3, and 5, which comprise 60% (3/5) of the subjects
with Grade B hepatic impairment.
In contrast to gepirone, both the AUC and Cmax for 1-PP in hepatically impaired
subjects were reduced significantly compared to healthy volunteers. The AUC and Cmax
were approximately one-half and one-third that in the healthy volunteers. In addition, the
metabolic ratio of AUCs for 1-PP indicated an approximate 4-fold reduction in the ability
of hepatically impaired patients to convert gepirone to 1-PP.

117

For 3’OH-gepirone, the AUC and Cmax were also lower in the hepatically
impaired group. The molar ratio of the AUC revealed a 2-fold reduction in the ability of
the hepatically impaired subjects to metabolize gepirone to 3’OH-gepirone.
Table 34. Comparison of 24-Hour Excretion of Gepirone and Its Metabolites in
Hepatically Impaired and Healthy Subjects.

In order to understand more fully the presence of metabolites, urine was analyzed
using the LC/MS assay (Table 34). The samples were hydrolyzed overnight, thus
resulting in total concentrations, free plus conjugated. The results showed that 60% of
the total analytes present in the urine consisted of 3, 5-dihydroxy gepirone. Also, the
amount of drug recovered in the urine during the first 24 h in severely impaired patients
was less than that of the other groups.
Conclusions.
Based on the individual patient data, Grade B (moderate) hepatic impairment
results in a decrease in the first pass metabolism of gepirone. As a result, hepatic
dysfunction can contribute to elevated and decrease levels of gepirone (2-fold elevation in
exposure) and 1-PP (3- and 2-fold reduction in Cmax and AUC), respectively. The
maximum daily dose of gepirone should not exceed 40 mg in moderately impaired
patients since the liver plays a major role in the metabolic pathway of the drug. The
pharmacokinetics of gepirone have not been adequately characterized in severe hepatic
impairment. It is not recommended that gepirone is administered to these patients.

18. Protocol 28708: A Single Dose Study to Assess the Effect of Renal Impairment

Objectives.
The objectives of this study were (1) to compare the pharmacokinetic parameters
of gepirone and its major metabolites (1-PP and 3’OH-gepirone) following a single dose

Samples were analyzed for gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH-gepirone by LC/MS/MS.

degrees of renal function as well as healthy subjects, were enrolled in the study. Thirtysix completed the study, consisting of four groups of 9/group. The subjects were

Table 37. Mean Pharmacokinetic Parameters of 1-PP.

Table 39. Mean Urine Gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH-gepirone Amounts.

Figure 33. Profile of Minor Metabolites in Urine.

For gepirone, an increased AUC by 65-71%, and consequently a decreased CLapp
by 60-62% were observed for the moderately and severely impaired patient groups (Fig.
30, Table 36). Also, a 74% increase in Cmax was observed in severely impaired subjects
compared with healthy subjects. No change in t1/2 was observed for gepirone among all
of the groups.
For 1-PP, an increased Cmax (by 79%), AUC (by 112-226%) and a prolonged t1/2
was observed in the severely impaired patients (Fig. 31, Table 37). As a result of the
increase in exposure, a 68% decrease in CLapp was observed in this group.
For 3’OH-gepirone, the results for the comparison of severe vs. healthy subjects
were similar to those for 1-PP, an increased Cmax, (by 50%), AUC (by 90-121%), and t1/2
(by 74%) (Fig. 32, Table 38). As a result, CLapp decreased by 52%. For the moderately
impaired patients, an increased t1/2 (by 38%) and a decreased CLapp (by 43%) were
observed.

124

Also, the amount of drug recovered in the urine during the first 24 h in severely
impaired patients was less than that of the other groups (Fig. 33, Table 39). Thus, these
findings support the changes in plasma pharmacokinetic parameters found in renal
impaired patients.
Conclusions.
Overall, plasma levels of gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH-gepirone increased with
respect to the level of renal impairment. The overall effect of renal insufficiency was
more pronounced for the metabolites and parent drug in the moderately and severely
impaired groups, resulting in prolonged exposure and half-lives. Thus, the maximum
daily dose should not exceed 40 mg in both moderate and severely impaired patients since
the kidneys play a major role in the elimination of the metabolites of gepirone.
19. Protocol 28717: Single and Multiple Dose Pharmacokinetic Interaction Study
Between Gepirone and Ketoconazole in Healthy Male Volunteers.
Investigator.
(b) (4)

Objectives.
The objectives of this study were (1) to compare the pharmacokinetics of gepirone
and its metabolites (1-PP and 3’OH-gepirone) after single and multiple dose
administration with and without pre-and co-administration of ketoconazole, and (2)
assess the tolerability of the combined administration of a single dose of gepirone and
ketoconazole.
Study Design and Methods:
This study consisted of an open-label, randomized, single and multiple dose, twoperiod crossover, design. Between gepirone dosing regimens, a 14-day washout phase
was implemented. Subjects were dosed in two groups according to the treatment
sequence:
1) Group 1: gepirone alone/gepirone + ketoconazole
2) Group 2: gepirone + ketoconazole/gepirone alone
Because of the high incidence of adverse events indicating a strong interaction between
ketoconazole and gepirone, the drug interaction was assessed with a single dose of
gepirone only. In addition, the gepirone dose that was administered with ketoconazole in
group 1 was reduced to 20 mg. The actual dosing schedule was according to the
following:
A)
A single dose of 40 mg gepirone on day 1 followed by a multiple dose regimen of
40 mg gepirone once daily on days 5-9. All 24 subjects received treatment A.

125

B1)
A single dose of 40 mg gepirone on day 1 with pre-and co-administration of 200
mg ketoconazole b.i.d. Dosing with ketoconazole started on the evening of day –3, and
continued up to an including the morning dose on day 1. Twelve subjects received this
treatment.
B2)
a single dose of 20 mg gepirone on day 1 with pre- and co-administration of 200
mg ketoconazole b.i.d. Dosing with ketoconazole commenced in the evening of day –3,
and continued up to and including the evening dose on day 4. Twelve subjects received
this treatment.
Doses were administered following a light meal except for days 1 and 9, when the
morning doses were given after a 10 h fasting period. Serial blood samples were
collected over a 96-h period on days 1 and 9. Extra samples (up to 192 h) were collected
after the single dose combination treatment in period 1. Urine was collected for 48 h after
the last gepirone dose in both periods. All samples were analyzed by a LC/MS assay to
determine the concentration of gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH-gepirone. In addition, during
both periods, a day 1 and 9 urine sample was collected for the determination of 6βhydroxycortisol as a marker for CYP3A4 inhibition.
Subjects:
Twenty-four healthy male volunteers (age range: 18 - 45 yrs; all Caucasians) were
enrolled and completed the study. All subjects received treatment A. 12 subjects
received treatment B1 and 12 subjects received treatment B2.
Test Product, Dose, and Mode of Administration:
(b) (4)
The test formulation of gepirone was supplied to the investigator by
, which included:
(b) (4)
• ER tablet formulation, product #
(Lot # 0581393), containing 20 mg of gepirone
HCl. Each subject received a total daily dose of 40 mg (2 x 20 mg tablets).
For ketoconazole (Nizoral®), the formulation was supplied by Janssen Pharmaceuticals,
which included:
• Tablet formulation (Lot # 98H07/356), containing 200 mg of ketoconazole. Each
subject was administered 200 mg b.i.d.
Assay Validation:
An LC/MS assay was used to quantitate gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH-gepirone in
this study. The calibration curves in plasma were linear for the concentration range from
0.1–10 ng/ml for gepirone, 0.2-5.0 ng/ml for 1-PP, and 0.25-10 ng/ml for 3’OH-gepirone
(r ≥ 0.988 for gepirone, r ≥ 0.984 for 1-PP, and r ≥ 0.989 for 3’OH-gepirone). Several
QC samples were prepared at concentrations of 0.15-8.0 ng/ml for gepirone, 0.6-4.0
ng/ml for 1-PP, and 0.75-8.0 ng/ml for 3’OH-gepirone. The deviations from the mean for
gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH were less than 13.2%, 17.6%, and 17.2%.
For urine, the calibration curves were linear for the concentration range from 2.5100 ng/ml for the low concentration range and 50-600 ng/ml for the high concentration

126

range for all analytes (r ≥ 0.995 for gepirone, r ≥ 0.991 for 1-PP, and r ≥ 0.986 for 3’OH).
Several QCs were prepared to cover the low and high concentration ranges, and as a
result the deviations from the mean for gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH were less than 13%,
25%, and 20%.
Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of various parameters
including a lag time (tlag), peak concentration (Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax),
half-life (t1/2), area under the curve (AUC0-t, ∝), minimum observed plasma concentration
(Cmin), steady-state pharmacokinetic parameters, and an accumulation ratio for AUC (day
8)/AUC (day 1). In addition, 6β-OHcortisol/cortisol ratios in urine were calculated.
Non-compartmental methods were used in the determination of various pharmacokinetic
parameters.
For Cmax and AUC, the parametric point estimates were calculated with 90%
confidence intervals derived from the ANOVA on log-transformed data. The ANOVA
incorporated sequence, subject, treatment, period, and residual error effects. Effects were
considered significant if p  0.05. In addition, a paired t-test was performed on the logtransformed AUC and t1/2.
Results.
Figure 34. Mean Single Dose Gepirone Curves.

Figure 35. Mean Single Dose 1-PP Curves.

Figure 36. Mean Single Dose 3’OH-gepirone Curves.

Figure 37. Mean Multiple Dose Gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH-gepirone Plasma Curves
after treatment with Gepirone Alone.

Table 40. Mean (SD) Single Dose Parameters for Gepirone.

129

Table 42. Mean (SD) Single Dose Parameters for 3’OH.

Table 43. Mean (SD) Multiple Dose Parameters after administration of Gepirone
Alone.

Table 44. Comparison between Predose and Single Dose/Multiple Dose 6βhydroxycortisol Ratios.

Following co-administration with ketoconazole, both the Cmax and AUC of
gepirone increased significantly by approximately 5- and 6-fold, respectively (Fig 34,
Table 40). As a result, the Cmax and AUC of 1-PP decreased approximately 2.5- and 2-

130

fold, respectively (Fig. 35, Table 41). Peak concentration s of both metabolites shifted to
a later time point (Figs. 35, 36, Tables 41, 42). Following multiple dose administration,
trough concentrations of gepirone and its metabolites did not increase (Fig. 37, Table 43).
Therefore, steady state conditions were achieved following one day of multiple dosing for
gepirone and 1-PP, and two days for 3’OH. For 1-PP, a significantly lower AUC and
Cmax was observed after multiple dose treatment of gepirone alone. In addition, coadministration of ketoconazole increased the amount of gepirone in urine 5-fold (data not
shown). The ratio of 6b-hyroxycortisol/cortisol in urine was significantly decreased after
co-administration with ketoconazole (Table 44).
Conclusions.
As a result of co-administration of gepirone with ketoconazole, gepirone plasma
concentrations increased 5-fold, while 1-PP concentrations decreased by approximately 3fold, in line with the metabolic formation of 1-PP by CYP3A4. Since gepirone and 3’OH
levels were elevated, a wide range of adverse events, including dizziness, nausea, and
vomiting, was observed following co-administration. In addition, the decrease in the 6βhyroxycortisol metabolic ratio was indicative of an inhibiting effect of gepirone on
CYP3A4. Thus, potential drug interactions may exist among concomitant medications
for the treatment of depression and gepirone, if the administered drugs are metabolized
via CYP3A4. As a result, gepirone administration is not warranted with ketoconazole.
Caution needs to be exercised and a gepirone dose reduction may be warranted when it is
co-administered with other CYP3A4 inhibitors.
20. Protocol FK-GBE-004: The Pharmacokinetics of Gepirone 20 mg MR Tablets
(b) (4)
(
) in Male Asians, Caucasians, and Blacks.
Objectives.
The objective of this study was to evaluate and compare the single and multiple
dose pharmacokinetics of gepirone in male Caucasians, Asians, and Blacks.
Study Design and Methods:
This study consisted of an open-label, single center, single and multiple dose
design. After an overnight fast, doses were administered in the morning on days 1 and 38 and no food was permitted until five h after administration. Serial blood samples were
collected over a 48-h period on days 1 and 8. A 24-h urine sample was also collected on
day 8. All samples were analyzed by a LC/MS assay to determine the concentration of
gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH gepirone.
Subjects:
Thirty-six healthy male volunteers (age range: 18 - 50 yrs; 12 Caucasians and 12
Blacks and 5 Asians) were enrolled in the study and 29 completed the study.

131

Test Product, Dose, and Mode of Administration:
(b) (4)
The test formulation of gepirone was supplied to the investigator by
, which included:
(b) (4)
• ER tablet formulation, product #
(Lot # 98-009T), containing 20 mg of gepirone
HCl. Each subjects received a total daily dose of 20 mg.
Assay Validation:
A LC/MS assay was used to quantitate gepirone, 1-PP, and 3’OH-gepirone in this
study. The calibration curves in plasma were linear for the concentration range from 0.1
– 4.5 ng/ml for gepirone, 0.10-9.0 ng/ml for 1-PP, and 0.25-10 ng/ml for 3’OH-gepirone
(r ≥ 0.995 for gepirone, r ≥ 0.995 for 1-PP, and r ≥ 0.994 for 3’OH-gepirone). Several
QC samples were prepared at concentrations of 0.5. 1.0, and 2.0 ng/ml for both gepirone
and 1-PP, and 0.25, 5.0, and 10 ng/ml for 3’OH-gepirone. The deviations from the mean
for gepirone were within 7.7% for the low QCs, 7.8% for the medium QCs, and 10.0%
for the high QCs. The corresponding values for 1-PP were 7.7%, 8.5%, and 8.8%,
respectively. The corresponding values for 3’OH-gepirone were 5.6%, 8.1%, and 9.6%,
respectively.
For urine, the calibration curves were linear for the concentration range from 2.575 ng/ml for both gepirone and 1-PP (r ≥ 0.991 and r ≥ 0.991).
Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of peak concentration
(Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax), half-life (t1/2), area under the curve (AUC0-12 h, ∝),
minimum observed plasma concentration (Cmin), and an accumulation ratio for AUC
(day 8)/AUC (day 1). Non-compartmental methods were used in the determination of
various pharmacokinetic parameters.
Statistical analysis was performed using a one-way ANOVA with group as the
main effect. Three bioequivalence ratios of least square means were constructed, treating
the Caucasian group as the reference, and the Asian and Black groups as test groups. The
lower and upper 90% confidence limits were calculated for each ratio.

132

Results.
Figure 38. Mean Plasma Concentrations (Day 1 and Day 8).

133

Table 45. Comparison of Mean Pharmacokinetics Results across Race on Day 1.

Table 46. Comparison of Mean Pharmacokinetic Parameters across Race on Day 8.

134

Table 47. Comparison of Mean AUC Metabolic Ratios.

During multiple dose administration, gepirone and its 3’OH metabolite
accumulated in all treatment groups (Fig. 38, Tables 45, 46). Similar accumulation was
observed for gepirone, but 3’OH gepirone accumulated more in Blacks and Caucasians
compared to Asian subjects. At steady state, trough levels were stable for the three
analytes. Following both single and multiple dose administration, the exposure of
gepirone was significantly greater in Caucasians (1.5-2-fold) compared to Blacks and
Asians. In addition, a trend for higher exposure of 1-PP was observed in Blacks and
Asians, but was not significantly different. The Black subjects had lower concentrations
of gepirone and higher concentrations of 1-PP following single and multiple dose
administration. The same trend was observed in Asian subjects, but the magnitude of the
difference was less compared to Caucasians. Also, these differences are reflected in the
values of each population’s metabolic ratios (Table 47).
Conclusions.
In conclusion, comparison of AUC values at steady-state and on day 1 revealed
that gepirone and 3’OH-gepirone accumulated in the plasma following multiple dose
administration, which were similar among all treatment groups. The data suggests that
the difference in the extent of gepirone bioavailability is due to a higher conversion of
gepirone to 1-PP in Black and Asian Subjects. No dose adjustment is recommended
based on race since patients will be dose-titrated to achieve optimal response.

21. Protocol CN105-085: Multiple Dose Pilot Study Evaluating the Safety,

Objectives.
The objectives of this study were to establish a safety and tolerability profile, and
evaluate the pharmacokinetics of gepirone extended release (ER) at a dosage of 120 mg

Study Design and Methods:
This study consisted of a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled design.
The study design included a 7-day treatment period. All subjects fasted the night prior to
dosing and were administered 120 mg of ER gepirone as specified in the protocol. The
subjects were allowed to consume food between 2-4 h following drug administration.
Serial blood samples were collected pre-dose and up to 24 h following gepirone
administration of the morning dose on days 1 and 7. Additional blood samples were
taken on days 3, 4, 5 and 6 to check the attainment of steady-state. All samples were
analyzed to determine the concentration of gepirone and 1-PP, one of the major
circulating metabolites.
Subjects:
Seventeen healthy male volunteers (age range: 18 - 40 yrs.; 11 Caucasian, 4
Black, 1 Hispanic and 1 Oriental) participated in the trial. Thirteen subjects received
gepirone ER and four received placebo.

mg (6 x 20 mg tablets).
(b) (4)
• Placebo tablet, product #
, was used to match the gepirone ER tablet.
Assay Validation:
A GC/MS assay was used to quantitate gepirone and 1-PP in this study. The
calibration curve was linear for the concentration range from 0.1 – 25 ng/ml for both
compounds (r ≥ 0.995). Multiple QC samples were prepared at concentrations ranging
from 1 – 40 ng/ml for both gepirone and 1-PP. For gepirone, the between day and within
day assay variability were less than 5.8% and 11.7%, respectively. The corresponding
values for 1-PP were 4.6% and 9.0%, respectively.

Four of 13 subjects that received gepirone withdrew from the study due to adverse
events, associated with dizziness and nausea. Thus, the pharmacokinetic parameters were
calculated based on 9 subjects.
Following drug administration, the AUC and Cmax values were increased
significantly for both the parent and metabolite on day 7 compared with day 1 (Table 49).
An apparent 63% and 30% accumulation in AUC for gepirone and 1-PP was observed
(Figs. 39, 40, Table 49). No statistical differences were observed for tmax and the
metabolic ratio between days 1 and 7. On days 1 and 7, the tmax values for gepirone and
1-PP were approximately 4 and 6 h, respectively. The trough concentrations for both
gepirone and 1-PP were variable, resulting in no clear trend following the first and last
dose (Table 48).
Conclusions.
As a result of 4 subjects withdrawing from the study, the 120 mg dose of gepirone
ER was not tolerated well. Steady-state conditions were achieved by the end of the
dosage interval for both the parent drug and metabolite. No accumulation in trough levels
was observed over the seven day treatment. The accumulation in AUC and Cmax values
appeared to be due to the characteristics of the extended release formulation, and not
saturation of metabolic pathways of gepirone.

23. Protocol CN105-007: Pharmacokinetics and Disposition of 14C-Gepirone in

Investigator.
(b) (4)

.

Objectives.
The objectives of this study were to describe the absorption, elimination and
pharmacokinetics of gepirone following IV and oral administration and to determine the
systemic bioavailability of the oral dose as unchanged drug and as total radiolabel.
Study Design and Methods:
This study consisted of an open, randomized, 3-period crossover design. This
study incorporated an initial session 1 to screen the potential subjects for adverse events
prior to admission of the subjects into the crossover segment (sessions 2, 3, and 4) of the
study (Table 51). Each subject qualified for study entry based on medical history and the
inclusion/exclusion criteria. All subjects fasted the night prior to dosing and were
administered gepirone as specified in the protocol. Serial blood samples were collected
pre-dose and up to 24 h following gepirone dosing for the determination of pertinent
pharmacokinetic parameters. Urine and feces were collected from all subjects in sessions
2, 3, and 4 at various time intervals up to 144 h post-dose. All samples were analyzed to
determine the content of gepirone and 1-PP, one of the major circulating metabolites.
Subjects:
Thirteen healthy male volunteers were recruited into session 1 of the study (age
range; 18-39 yrs) (Table 51). Of the 13 subjects, 9 subjects who exhibited the least side
effects were selected to continue into sessions 2, 3, and 4 of the 3-way crossover segment
of the study. This approach was used since emesis is a common adverse effect associated
with gepirone administration. These 9 subjects completed all 4 sessions of the study.
Test Product, Dose, and Mode of Administration:
The 14C-gepirone HCL, lots #27017-15B and 27017-17B, which were used in the
preparation of the intravenous and oral solution formulations, respectively, were labeled
in the 2-position of the pyrimidinyl moiety.
The formulations of gepirone hydrochloride that were supplied to the investigator
by the Pharmaceutical Product Development Division of Bristol-Myers Squibb Co.
include:

µCi/ml. In the study, each subject received a total dose of 1 mg IV equivalent to 12.4
µCi. The radiochemical purity of this solution was 97.5%.

Criteria for Evaluation:

The results of this study indicated that independent of the route of administration,
the dose of gepirone was accounted for quantitatively in both urine and feces (data not
shown). After IV dosing, 89% of the dose was recovered in urine and 13.3% in feces 6

145

days after dosing. Following oral administration, 80.8% and 13% of the dose was
recovered in urine and feces, respectively. The majority of the radioactivity was
eliminated in urine within 24 h and urinary and fecal elimination was essentially complete
within 96 h following administration of the drug. The percentage of radioactivity in
plasma that was unchanged gepirone at any time after oral administration was less than
9% and after IV administration, it was 50%. Gepirone was extensively metabolized
following oral administration, resulting in a low oral bioavailability of 14–17% (Table
52). The parent drug exhibited a large volume of distribution, a high rate of systemic
clearance, and a relatively short half-life (2.6-2.9 h) (Figs 42, 43, Table 52). In contrast to
gepirone, 1-PP had a longer t1/2 and MRT of approximately 6 and 9 h, respectively. In
addition, the AUC for 1-PP is 3-fold greater than that of gepirone.
Conclusions.
Following administration of 14C-gepirone both orally and intravenously, the total
radioactivity was recovered independent of the route of administration. Gepirone was
subjected to an extensive 1st pass effect following oral absorption, resulting in a poor
bioavailability. In addition, the parent drug exhibited a large volume of distribution and a
high systemic clearance, with values approaching that of hepatic blood flow.
24. Protocol CN105-025: Single and Multiple Dose Pharmacokinetics of Gepirone IR
in Poor and Extensive Metabolizers of Dextromethorphan.
Objectives.
The objective of this study was to evaluate and compare the single and multiple
dose pharmacokinetics of gepirone and 1-PP following oral administration of gepirone to
male subjects phenotyped as poor or extensive metabolizers of dextromethorphan.
Study Design and Methods:
This study consisted of an open-label, single and multiple dose, parallel group
design. After an overnight fast, both groups received a single 15 mg dose of gepirone on
day 1 followed by a collection of blood samples for 48 h. On days 3 through 7, all
subjects received 15 mg of gepirone every 12 h. On day 8, only the morning dose was
administered followed by a collection of blood samples over 48 h. Additional blood
samples were collected prior to the morning dose on days 3-7 for determining Cmin
values. All samples were analyzed by a GC/MS assay to determine the concentration of
gepirone and 1-PP.
Subjects:
Twelve subjects (6 poor and 6 extensive metabolizers of dextromethorphan) (age
range: 18 - 35 yrs; 11 Caucasians and 1 Asian) were enrolled in the study and divided into
two groups.

146

Test Product, Dose, and Mode of Administration:
The test formulation of gepirone was supplied to the investigator by Bristol-Myers
Squibb Co., which included:
(b) (4)
• IR capsule formulation, product #
, was a no. 3 capsule containing 5 mg
of gepirone HCl. Each subjects received a total dose of 15 mg (3 x 5 mg) b.i.d. for
eight days.
Assay Validation:
A GC/MS assay was used to quantitate gepirone and 1-PP in this study. The
calibration curve was linear for the concentration range from 0.1 – 25 ng/ml for both
compounds (r ≥ 0.991 for gepirone and r ≥ 0.998 for 1-PP). Several QC samples were
prepared at concentrations ranging from 2.12-104 ng/ml for both gepirone and 1-PP. The
between day and within day variability for the QC samples did not exceed 6.2% and
17.3% for gepirone and 11.8% and 3.8% for 1-PP.
Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of peak concentration
(Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax), half-life (t1/2), area under the curve (AUC0-12 h, ∝),
minimum observed plasma concentration (Cmin), and an accumulation ratio for AUC
(day 8)/AUC (day 1). Non-compartmental methods were used in the determination of
various pharmacokinetic parameters.
Due to the small sample size, no formal statistical analysis was performed. Only a
paired t-test (p < 0.05) was used to compare the accumulation ratios of poor and extensive
metabolizers.

147

Following drug administration, the plasma concentration of gepirone reached a
peak level between 1 and 2 h (Fig. 44, Table 53). The plasma concentrations of both
analytes were similar in characteristics, but higher on day 8 than on day 1. Neither Cmax
nor AUC for gepirone differed between the poor and extensive metabolizers on days 1
and 8. Both AUC and Cmax were increased on day 8, with an accumulation ratio of 1.31
for poor metabolizers and 1.37 for extensive metabolizers of dextromethorphan. In

149

addition, trough concentrations for gepirone were relatively stable between days 3-8 (Fig.
3).
For 1-PP, poor metabolizers had both an increased AUC and Cmax compared to
extensive metabolizers (Fig. 45, Table 53). The increase in AUC for poor metabolizers
on days 1 and 8 was approximately 2.5 fold. Mean trough concentrations in the poor
metabolizer group were about 3-fold higher than those of the extensive metabolizer group
(Fig. 46).
Conclusions.
No change was observed in Cmax and AUC for gepirone in subjects with reduced
activity of CYP2D6, but a 2.5 fold increase was shown in the AUC for 1-PP. As a result,
poor metabolizers of dextromethorphan compensated for the reduced metabolism of
gepirone through the 2D6 pathway with an increase in the conversion of gepirone to 1-PP
probably via the 3A4 metabolic route.
25. Protocol CN105-012: Gepirone Site of Gastrointestinal Absorption in Man.
Investigator.
(b) (4)

Objectives.
The objectives of this study were (1) to use an intubation method for dosing
gepirone to the region of interest of the GI tract, (2) to assess and compare the absorption
of gepirone from the distal and proximal regions of the intestines to that of an oral
solution dose, and (3) to determine and compare the bioavailability of gepirone when
given as 4 consecutive doses of 5 mg at hourly intervals or as a single 20 mg dose.
Study Design and Methods:
This was an open study in which healthy male volunteers were administered three
single doses of 20 mg gepirone HCL and a 20 mg dose divided into 4 consecutive doses
of 5 mg each given every hour. Each subject qualified for study entry based on medical
history and the inclusion/exclusion criteria. The study was carried over 4 sessions, with a
1 week washout between doses. All subjects fasted the night prior to drug administration.
The subjects were allowed to eat lunch at 5 h following dosing. In session 1, 9 subjects
received a 20 mg dose of gepirone and then they were subsequently screened for potential
side effects prior to admission into the intubation segments (sessions 2 and 3) of the
study. Seven subjects received 20 mg of gepirone into the distal segment of the intestine
in session 2 and the proximal segment of the GI tract in session 3. In session 4, the seven
subjects received 4 consecutive doses of 5 mg one hour apart. Serial blood and urine
samples were collected for 30 h following drug administration. All samples were
analyzed to determine the content of gepirone and 1-PP.

150

Subjects:
Nine healthy male volunteers (age range: 19 –36 yrs; weight range: 65-91 kg)
were recruited into session 1 and as a result, the 7 subjects that showed the best tolerance
to the drug were selected to complete all 4 sessions.
Test Product, Dose, and Mode of Administration:
The formulations of gepirone oral solution that were supplied to the investigator
by the Pharmaceutical Product Development Division of Bristol-Myers Squibb Co.
include:
1. Gepirone HCl oral solution: containing 1 mg/ml, packaged 5 ml in a 10 ml vial,
(b) (4)
product #
.
2. Gepirone HCl oral solution: containing 1 mg/ml, packaged 20 ml in a 30 ml vial,
(b) (4)
product #
.

Assay Validation:
A GC/MS assay was used to quantitate gepirone and 1-PP in this study. The
sponsor stated that each analytical run resulted in two output files containing the results
for either gepirone or 1-PP. For each run, the calibration curves were linear for the
concentration range from 0.1 – 25 ng/ml for both compounds (r  0.995 for gepirone and
r  0.997 for 1-PP). Low and high QC samples were prepared at concentrations of 1.98
and 18.8 ng/ml for both gepirone and 1-PP. Another QC sample of 97.8 ng/ml was also
prepared for samples that were diluted. The between day variability for the QC samples
in all runs did not exceed 8.4% for gepirone and 10.4% for 1-PP. For the accuracy of the

151

method, the deviations from the mean for gepirone did not exceed 6.3% for the low QC
sample and 6.5% for the high QC sample. For 1-PP, the deviations from the mean were
10.8% for the low QC sample and 7.34% for the high QC sample.
Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of peak concentration
(Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax), area under the curve (AUC0-), half-life (t1/2) and
mean residence time (MRT).
The effect of site/treatment on Cmax, MRT, t1/2, and AUC was evaluated by a
randomized block ANOVA, with subjects as the blocks, including a residual error term
for both gepirone and 1-PP. Tukey’s multiple comparisons procedure was used at the
α=0.05 level.
Results.

1-4 in Subject # (6)

Figure 48. Mean Plasma Concentrations of 1-PP Following Gepirone Dosing in
(b)
Sessions 1-4 in Subject # (6)

154

Following administration into the distal portion of the GI tract (Treatment 2)
(Figs. 47, 48, Table 55), both Cmax and AUC of gepirone and 1-PP were significantly
greater than treatments 1 and 3 (proximal GI). In contrast, no differences in AUC or Cmax
were observed between treatments 1 and 3. The half-lives for gepirone and 1-PP were
similar across all treatment regimens. The formation of 1-PP was rapid, indicated by its
short tmax and the rate of metabolite formation was similar across all treatments.
In addition, treatment 4 was designed to mimic a sustained release dosage form.
When this regimen was compared with treatment 1, the overall amount of drug absorbed
was similar, thus suggesting that the bioavailability of an ER dosage form would not be
affected.
Conclusions.
Gepirone was absorbed throughout the entire length of the small intestine and no
apparent specific window of absorption exists. Thus, GI absorption of gepirone should
not be affected when the drug is released from an extended release dosage form.
26. Protocol CN105-005: Pharmacokinetics of Gepirone IR Following Single and
Multiple Dosing in Young Adult and Elderly Men and Women.
Objectives.
The objective of this study was to evaluate differences in gepirone single and
multiple dose pharmacokinetics that may be associated with the subject’s age and /or
gender.
Study Design and Methods:
This study consisted of an open-labeled, parallel group design. All subjects fasted
the night prior to dosing. On days 1 and 7, the subjects were administered 10 mg of drug
(Table 56). On days 3-6, the subjects were dosed with 10 mg t.i.d. Blood and urine
samples were collected over 48 h on days 1 and 7. Single blood samples also were
collected for Cmin determinations immediately prior to the morning dose on days 4 and 7.
Samples were analyzed for gepirone and 1-PP by GC/MS. Urine samples were not
analyzed.

Subjects:

Test Product, Dose, and Mode of Administration:
The test formulation of gepirone IR tablets was supplied to the investigator by
Bristol-Myers Squibb Co., which included:
(b) (4)
• IR capsule formulation, product #
(Lot #E87C071), was a no. 0 gray,

Assay Validation:
A GC/MS assay was used to quantitate both gepirone and 1-PP in this study. The
calibration curve was linear for the concentration range from 0.1 – 25 ng/ml for both
gepirone (r ≥ 0.991) and 1-PP (r ≥ 0.996). Low, medium, and high QC samples were
prepared at concentrations of 2.2, 18.4 and 97.6 ng/ml for gepirone and 2.3, 19.5 and 97.6
ng/ml for 1-PP. The between day variability for the QC samples did not exceed 6.38%
for gepirone and 6.40% for 1-PP. For the accuracy of the method, the deviations from the
mean for gepirone were within 8.80 % for the low QCs, 7.825 for the medium QCs and
2.63% for the high QCs. The corresponding values for 1-PP were 7.22%, 4.20 and
1.12%, respectively.
Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of peak concentration
(Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax), area under the curve (AUC0-t), half-life (t1/2),
trough concentrations (Cmin), mean residence times (MRT), and an accumulation ratio
(R).
Non-compartmental methods were used in the determination of various
pharmacokinetic parameters. Assuming constant CL and Vd during the multiple dosing
period, gepirone and 1-PP plasma concentrations were extrapolated to steady-state from
single dose data obtained on day 1 by the method of superpositioning.

156

In comparing the single and multiple dose pharmacokinetics, repeated measures
ANOVA was utilized to compare the Cmax, Cmin, t1/2, MRT0-", AUC, tmax (rank
transformed) and the metabolic ratio (MR). With the exception of Levene’s test
(p=0.001), which was used to test homogeneity of variance for t1/2 and MRT, the
significance level was set at p=0.05. In addition, a t-statistic was used to evaluate the
predictability of steady-state values from single dose data.
Results.
Figure 49. Mean Plasma Gepirone Concentrations on Day 1.

157

Figure 50. Mean Plasma Gepirone Concentrations on Day 7.

Figure 51. Mean Plasma Concentrations of 1-PP on Day 1.

158

Figure 52. Mean Plasma Concentrations of 1-PP on Day 10.

159

Table 57. Mean Pharmacokinetic Parameters of Gepirone.

160

Table 58. Mean Pharmacokinetic Parameters of 1-PP.

For gepirone, tmax was approximately 1 h, indicating that absorption was rapid,
and independent of the multiple dosing regimen (Figs. 49, 50, Table 57). The t1/2 ranged
from 2.7-4.6 h following single and multiple dose administration. Based on Cmin, Cmax,
and AUC data, steady-state conditions were achieved after 2 days of t.i.d. dosing. Cmin
values were unchanged for both gepirone and 1-PP. Cmax and AUCss on day 7 were
significantly greater than the values on day 1 in all groups, indicating some accumulation
of gepirone following multiple dosing. As a result, plasma concentrations of gepirone
accumulated less than 3-fold. Cmax, AUC, and MRT values were greater in the elderly
subjects. Also, male subjects had greater values for Cmax and AUC (15-25% elevation)
compared with females.
For 1-PP, plasma levels were observed within 15 min (Figs. 51, 52, Table 58).
The t1/2 ranged from 5-8 h following single and multiple dose administration. For all
groups, the pharmacokinetics on day 7 were greater than the values on day 1, illustrating
some accumulation of 1-PP. In contrast to gepirone pharmacokinetics, no significant age
or gender effects were observed for Cmax, AUC, and t1/2. No change in the metabolic ratio
was observed in all dose groups.

161

Conclusions.
The results of this study showed that significant differences in gepirone
pharmacokinetics were obtained based on age and/or gender as well as multiple dosing.
Following multiple dose administration, plasma levels accumulated less than 3-fold. The
AUC, Cmax and MRT values were augmented in the elderly subjects after single dose and
at steady-state. Thus, a dose reduction is warranted when treating the elderly with
gepirone.

Following drug administration, gepirone was absorbed rapidly, indicated by a
short tmax of 0.7 – 1.9 h (Fig. 53, Table 59). Tmax remained unchanged independent of
dose for both gepirone and 1-PP (Figs 53, 54, Table 59). The parent drug also was
metabolized rapidly, due to observed plasma levels of 1-PP within 15 min (Fig. 54).
Following administration of the 30 and 60 mg doses, the half-lives for gepirone and 1-PP
could not be determined. Thus, the t1/2 following the 100 mg dose was 2.3 h and 5.1 h for
gepirone and 1-PP, respectively. A trend for nonlinearity at the higher doses was
observed between dose and AUC, but was inconclusive based on the AUC values for
both compounds only representing plasma levels up to 5 h. As a result, the AUC up to 5
h represented 77% and 34% of the total AUC for gepirone and 1-PP, respectively.
Conclusions.
Overall, gepirone was well tolerated up to 80 mg. The results showed that
gepirone was rapidly absorbed and metabolized following oral administration. Even
though the sponsor stated that gepirone and 1-PP may exhibit nonlinear pharmacokinetics
at higher doses, this assessment cannot be adequately interpreted due to a statistical
comparison of AUC values only up to 5 h.

A radioimmunoassay (RIA) was used to quantitate gepirone and a GC/MS assay
was used for 1-PP in this study. The calibration curve was linear for the concentration
range from 0.1 – 5 ng/ml for gepirone (r ≥ 0.992) and 0.2 – 25 ng/ml for 1-PP (r ≥ 0.997).
Low, medium, and high QC samples were prepared at concentrations of 0.4, 3.0, and 25
ng/ml for gepirone and 0.99, 11.9, and 21.8 ng/ml for 1-PP. The between day variability
for the QC samples did not exceed 9% for gepirone and 7.25% for 1-PP. For the
accuracy of the method, the deviations from the mean for gepirone were within 8.69% for
the low QCs, 7.98% for the middle QCs, and 5.20% for the high QCs. The corresponding
values for 1-PP were 6.57%, 6.70%, and 4.05%, respectively.
Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of peak concentration
(Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax), and area under the curve (AUC0-8 h). Noncompartmental methods were used in the determination of various pharmacokinetic
parameters.
A paired t-test was used to compare Cmax values of gepirone and 1-PP for the 10
mg dose level. AUC and Cmax were evaluated by linear regression analysis to determine
if these parameters were both linear and proportional to dose following multiple doses of
gepirone. An ANOVA also was used to test linearity and proportionality to evaluate
AUC and Cmax normalized to a 1 mg dose. Comparisons were made at each dose level
using the Tukey-Kramer multiple comparison procedure and significance was determined
at the α = 0.05 level.

167

Following drug administration, gepirone was absorbed rapidly, indicated by a
short tmax of 0.8 – 1.3 h (Fig. 55, Table 61). Tmax remained unchanged independent of
dose for both gepirone and 1-PP (Figs. 55, 56, Table 61). The parent drug also was
metabolized rapidly, due to observed plasma levels of 1-PP within 15 min (Fig. 56).
Following administration of the 10, 25, and 50 mg doses, Cmax was dose proportional for
both gepirone and 1-PP. A trend for linearity was apparent between dose and AUC for
both gepirone and 1-PP, and statistical analysis revealed that no significant mean
differences existed between the normalized values.
Conclusions.
Following multiple dose administration, gepirone was absorbed and metabolized
rapidly independent of dose, indicated by the short tmax values. As a result, both gepirone
and 1-PP exhibited dose linear pharmacokinetics up to 50 mg.
11. Protocol 59B4A-001-2579: A Pharmacokinetic Study of Gepirone IR After
Single and Multiple Dosing in Normal Male Subjects.
Objectives.
The objective of this study was to provide a more definitive picture regarding the
single and multiple dose pharmacokinetics of gepirone and 1-PP in healthy male
volunteers.

169

Study Design and Methods:
This study consisted of an open-labeled, placebo-controlled, double-blind, parallel
group design. All subjects fasted the night prior to dosing and up to 5 h following
administration. On days 1 and 10, the subjects were administered 5, 10, 20, or 30 mg of
drug (Table 62). On days 3-9, the subjects were dosed t.i.d. with the dose they received
on day 1. Blood samples were collected over 48 h on days 1 and 10, and for 8 h on day 7.
Single blood samples also were collected for Cmin determinations immediately prior to the
morning dose on days 4, 5, 6, 8, and 9. Samples were analyzed for gepirone and 1-PP by
GC/MS. Subjects in the 30 mg group (group 3) discontinued treatment following the
second dose due to adverse events. Thus, comparisons at steady-state were made using
the data from the 5, 10, and 20 mg groups.

Subjects:
Forty-two healthy male volunteers (age range: 18 - 40 yrs., 28 Caucasian, 14
Black) were enrolled in the study. All groups received gepirone, but only groups 1, 2,
and 3, a total of 10 subjects/group completed the study. Thirteen subjects, including all
of group 4, discontinued before the completion of the study.
Test Product, Dose, and Mode of Administration:
The test formulation of gepirone IR tablets was supplied to the investigator by
Bristol-Myers Squibb Co., which included:

(Lot #E85F129), continued no active
ingredients.
Each subject received 6 capsules at every dosing period, which were made up of a
combination of test drug and placebo.

170

Assay Validation:
A GC/MS assay was used to quantitate both gepirone and 1-PP in this study. The
calibration curve was linear for the concentration range from 0.1 – 25 ng/ml for both
gepirone (r ≥ 0.995) and 1-PP (r ≥ 0.993). Low and high QC samples were prepared at
concentrations of 2.0 and 20 ng/ml for gepirone and 2.12 and 21.2 ng/ml for 1-PP. The
between day variability for the QC samples did not exceed 9.86% for gepirone and 5.55%
for 1-PP. For the accuracy of the method, the deviations from the mean for gepirone were
within 7.70 % for the low QCs and 8.90% for the high QCs. The corresponding values
for 1-PP were 5.29% and 5.72%, respectively.
Criteria for Evaluation:
Pharmacokinetic evaluation included the determination of peak concentration
(Cmax), corresponding peak times (tmax), area under the curve (AUC0-t), half-life (t1/2),
trough concentrations (Cmin), and an accumulation ratio (R). Non-compartmental
methods were used in the determination of various pharmacokinetic parameters.
In comparing the single and multiple dose pharmacokinetics, repeated measures
ANOVA was utilized to compare the Cmin, t1/2, MRT0-", AUC0-", tmax (rank transformed)
and AUC0-8 data. With the exception of Levene’s test (p=0.001), which was used to test
homogeneity of variance for t1/2 and MRT, the significance level was set at p=0.05.
Weighted linear regression was used to determine whether Cmin, Cmax, and AUC were
linear or proportional to dose. Tests for linearity were conducted using the lack-of-fit tstatistic. Due to discontinuation of the 30 mg group following the second dose, the data
only was included in the assessment of dose proportionality.

Results.
Figure 57. Mean Plasma Gepirone Concentrations on Day 1.

Figure 58. Mean Plasma Gepirone Concentrations on Day 10.

172

Figure 59. Mean Plasma Concentrations of 1-PP on Day 1.

Figure 60. Mean Plasma Concentrations of 1-PP on Day 10.

AVAILABLE
COPY

173

Table 63. Mean Pharmacokinetic Parameters of Gepirone.

174

Table 64. Mean Pharmacokinetic Parameters of 1-PP.

175

For gepirone, tmax ranged from 0.7 – 1.1 h, indicating that absorption was rapid,
similar among doses, and independent of the multiple dosing regimen (Figs. 57, 58, Table
63). The t1/2 ranged from 2 –3.5 h following single and multiple dose administration.
Based on Cmin, Cmax, and AUC data, steady-state conditions were achieved after 2 days of
t.i.d. dosing. As a result, plasma concentrations of gepirone accumulated 1.5-1.9 fold.
Also, AUCss on day 7 was significantly greater than the AUC on day 1. After a single
dose of gepirone, Cmax and AUC were linear with respect to dose up to 30 mg and 20 mg,
respectively. At steady-state, both Cmax and AUC were linear with respect to dose up to
20 mg, but the outcome is marginal due to high variability in the data.
For 1-PP, plasma levels were observed within 15 min (Figs. 59, 60). The t1/2
ranged from 5-8 h following single and multiple dose administration (Table 64). Based
on Cmax and AUC data, plasma concentrations of 1-PP accumulated approximately 1.7
fold. In contrast to gepirone pharmacokinetics, the AUCss of 1-PP was similar to the
AUC on day 1, thus suggesting that the rate of metabolite formation did not change as a
result of multiple dosing of the parent compound. Both Cmax and AUC were linear with
respect to dose up to 20 mg following single and multiple dose administration, but the
data was associated with high variability.
Conclusions.
Gepirone was absorbed and metabolized rapidly independent of dose, indicated by
the short tmax values. Steady state plasma levels were obtained after 2 days of t.i.d.
dosing. Following multiple dose administration, both gepirone and 1-PP profiles showed
little accumulation (approximately 2-fold), which may not be clinically relevant. In
addition AUC and Cmax were linear with respect to dose up to 20 mg for both gepirone
and 1-PP, but the data was highly variable. As a result of the method of superpositioning,
predicted values of AUCss and Cmin were significantly less that the values observed,
resulting in that steady state pharmacokinetics of gepirone and 1-PP cannot be predicted
from single dose data. One possible explanation may be due to high variability in the
data or saturation of metabolic pathways at higher doses.

Background:
Gepirone was developed initially by Bristol-Myers Squibb, Inc., but a business decision led to its
termination. In 1998, Organon signed an agreement with Fabre-Kramer Pharmaceuticals to further develop
the drug. During development, Organon had initially submitted an NDA for the extended-release formulation
of gepirone, but the agency refused to file the NDA because the sponsor did not have two positive wellcontrolled efficacy trials. As a result, Organon has re-submitted the NDA for approval in the treatment of
depression.
Gepirone is an azapirone derivative, which has both antidepressant and anxiolytic properties by
interacting with serotonin (5-HT)1A receptors. The drug is metabolized by both CYP450 3A4 and 2D6, thus
producing the potential for a variety of drug interactions. In the current submission, the effect of inducers of
various CYP450 isozymes has not been studied. The 20 mg strength is the only strength that has been used in
the clinical trials, although the sponsor wants to market 20, 40, 60, and 80 mg strengths. As a result, a
bioequivalency study has been submitted linking the 20, 40, and 80 mg strengths. In addition, gepirone has
encountered several formulation changes throughout the drug development process.

Comments

We are requesting that the following comments be sent to the sponsor:
1.

The sponsor has performed a Bioequivalence study comparing the 20,
40, and 80 mg strengths. They intend to also market a 60 mg
strength. The sponsor needs to provide dissolution data comparing
the 60 mg tablet to other strengths and justification for not testing the
60 mg strength in vivo.

2.

The sponsor has included a list of all clinical pharmacology studies to
date, but has not clearly indicated which extended release formulation
was used in each study. Please provide an amended table to include
which BMS or (b) (4) formulation was used in each study (ex. ER-1,
ER-2, ER-3, ER-4, and (b) (4) ER formulation).

3.

Electronic submission of data for all clinical pharmacology,
pharmacokinetic, and dose-response studies is needed.

4.

Need dissolution method development report, and data for each of the
ER strengths to be marketed. Also, the pH-solubility profile for
gepirone is needed. We recognize that this information may have
been submitted in another volume of the NDA. Please submit hard
copies and indicate the section of the electronic NDA where the
information resides.

1.

2.

impairment)?
